PREFACE. 

This volume completes the official records of the original General 
Baptists down to 1811. During the nineteenth century they issued their 
Minutes annually in print, and those whose interest has been awakened 
will have little difficulty in following up the story. 

These volumes bring out clearly two points, as to government and 
as to doctrine. The General Baptists inherited from the continental 
Anabaptists the system of government which the Calvinists also adopted, 
and which has become so well known! as Presbyterian; a system of 
graded courts all controlled by the General Assembly. A futile attempt 
was made by the Long Parliament to force this on the Established Church, 
in the very years when the English General Baptists framed their organi- 
zation. By 1660 it broke down finally in the Establishment, by 1680 
many General Baptists doubted its wisdom, more challenged it in 1697, 
and despite brave assertions that Independence was dangerous, a partial 
surrender was made in 171 1. In practice the control has long been 
abandoned, but the old affinities show themselves in that the General 
Baptists even to-day are on friendly terms with the survivors of the 
Pasdobaptist Presbyterians of 1662. The Society of Friends, which at its 
origin was so closely related to the General Baptists, and retained longer 
those peculiarities of attire, marriage, worship, and doctrine which were 
common to both, does yet maintain a similar system of organization. 
And the records of its Quarterly and Yearly meetings, dating from the 
same early period, deserve equally to be put before the world. 

In the matter of doctrine, the " General " Baptists enshrine in their 
title the declaration that the grace of God is available generally, for all 
men, and not for some only. When tjiey first said this, they were as 
voices in a wilderness, for in a Calvinistic atmosphere the Articles of 
Religion had been revised for England, and the Five Points were 
sharpened at Dort just as John Smith challenged their peculiarities. The 
Midland Confession of 165I must be read with the recollection of the 
Westminster Confession of 1646. Its companion Shorter Catechism is 
better known, and shows what the great Puritan party then held. Not 
only did mankind lack original righteousness, and suffer from corruption 
of the whole nature, but it was involved in the guilt of Adam's first sin, 
it was under God's wrath and curse, liable to all miseries in this life, to 
death itself, and to all the pains of hell for ever. The remedy for this 
was only of limited application, according to the Puritan theology, which 
announced that God out of His mere good pleasure, from all eternity, 
elected some to everlasting life. Against this ambiguity the General 
Baptists quoted scripture that God wished to redeem (not some, but) all 
men. And this was their cardinal doctrine. 

Most of the bodies which still pay deference to the Westminstei: 
standards have declared their faith anew^ and the contention of the 
General Baptists has long been widely accepted, to the humanising of 
theology, and the revival of practical Christian efiFort for the salvation 
of men. 

W. T. WHITLEY.

Wycliffite Version, about 1380. 

Whosoever weakens or abuses this sign does violence to the words 
of Christ. The significance of this sign and symbol, the obligation of 
fidelity even unto death in hope of a resurrection to a future life, is 
moreover of greater moment than the sign itself. But these significant 
things can have no applicability to infants. Therefore the baptism of 
infants is foliage without vintage. 

Hubmaier, 1525. 

[Baptism is] a rite of initiation wTiereby men, after admitting his 
doctrine and embracing faith in him, are bound to Christ and planted 
among his disciples, or in his church. 

Racovian Catechism, 1590, 1605. 

Original sin is an idle term, and there is no such thing as men 
intend by the word. . . . God doth not create or predestinate any 
man to destruction. ... As God created all men according to his 
image, so hath He redeemed all that fall by actual sin, to the same end. 
. . . All penitent and faithful Christians are brethren in the com- 
munion of the outward church, wheresoever they live, by what name 
soever they are known, which in truth and zeal, follow repentance and 
faith. . . . The outward baptism of water, is to be admmistered only 
upon such penitent and faithful persons as are (aforesaid), and not upon 
innocent infents, or wicked persons. . . . The outward baptism and 
supper do not confer, and convey grace and regeneration to the partici- 
pants and communicants. . . . The magistrate is not by virtue of 
his office to meddle with religion, or matters of conscience, to force or 
compel men to this or that form of religion, or doctrine. 

John Smith's Propositions, about 161 1; ,18, 25, 27, 69, 70, 73, 84. 

By the promised seed of the woman, Jesus Christ his obedience, all 
are made righteous, all are made alive : His righteousness being imputed 
unto all. . . . The king is a mortall man, & not God therefore hath 
no power over ye immortall soules of his subiects, to make lawes & 
ordinances for them, and to set spirituall Lords over them. 

Thomas Helwys, i6ii and 1612. 

Jesus Christ, through the grace of God, suffered death for all man- 
kind, or every man. . . . The second Adam will as certainly raise 
all mankind from that death which fell on them, through or by the 
first Adam's sin or offence, as surely as they partake of it. . . . Jesus 
Christ was not only the Lawmaker, but the Law giver to every man that 
liveth in the world, in that he giveth every man therein some measure 
of light. . . . The Baptisme which the Lord Jesus commanded his 
disciples to teach, ought to be known by every one, before they submit 
themselves, or obey it. 

Midland Confession of 1651 : Articles 17, 18, 28, 47.

The West Midland churches dropped out of touch by 1695 ; the Lincoln- 
shire churches had a vigorous Association life, and are responsible for work 
over their borders in Yorks, Notts, and Norfolk ; the Northants churches with 
Leicester and Warwick kept up the traditions of j6si. In practice the 
Assembly was almost a Home Counties body. 



GLEANINGS, 1652— 1680. 



Amongst the original Letters and Papers of State 
addressed to Oliver Cromwell, 1649 — 1658, in the collection 
of John Milton, is a letter of i January, 165 1-2, which supple- 
ments the " Faith and Practice of Thirty Congregations " in 
the Midlands, by supplying details of thirteen churches in 
and around Staffordshire, to which allusion was made in 
Volume I., page 30. The letter was reprinted by Mr. John 
Taylor of Northampton, and his reprint has been placed at 
our disposal by the Rev. A. S. Langley, F.R.Hist.S^, of 
Longton in Staffordshire. The following churches and names 
must be added to the lists in Volume I.., pages xxxv and Iviii. 



DERBY 
Parwich 

LEICESTER 
Bitteswell 
Gumley [or Thedding- 

worth]

Ravensthorpe

" Berryhill " 
Burton-on-Trent 
Lichfield 
Shugborough

John Row, John Tomlinson. 

Thomas Morris, Thomas Townsend. 

William Burditt, John Coles. 
Conyars Congreve, Thomas Rogers. 
Robert Fielding, Edward Smith. 

Francis Stanley, Robert Teaslow. 

James Brown, Thomas Jefferyes.

Robert Clarke, Thomas Wright. 
Robert Prittie, Francis Silvester.

We may conjecture that these churches arose under the 
same impulse that produced the Midland churches which drew 
up the Faith and Practise; indeed one or two belong to both 
groups. But these seem to find their centre of gravity in 
Stafford, a military post, whose governor was Henry 



xii. GLEANINGS, 1652-1680 

D'Anvers. In Volume I. he was classed as General Baptist, 
and as late as 1656 he was associated with Samuel Gates; 
but from 1673 onwards his associations were entirely with 
the Particular Baptists, and he wrote forcibly against the 
Laying on of Hands; hence his name should probably be 
removed from our lists at page xliv. 

Unexpected confirmation has come to the conjecture on 
page 24 of Volume I., that Assemblies could be held from 
1672. C. R. Haines, Esq., M.A., printed in 1899 for private 
circulation a complete memoir of his ancestor, Richard 
Haines, 1633 — 1685, who lived at SuUington in Sussex, and 
was a member of the Horsham church. A copy of this has 
been lent by the author, who justly claims that we shall find 
in it a full account of an episode of great importance in 
the history, nowhere else described in detail. It shows that 
Assemblies were held from 1673 to 1680, and implies that 
they were the rule, not the exception. It throws a strange 
light on the subjects that Assemblies would then deal with, 
on organization and on procedure. In our lamentable 
ignorance of the Assembly's doings before 1689, the episode 
may be briefly retold. 

Richard Haines became a Baptist after 12 June, 1656, 
when his eldest child was baptized, and probably before 29 
October, 1657, when he allowed his second child to die un- 
baptized, all subsequent children also being unbaptized. 
Matthew Caffin lived at Broadbridge, in the same parish, and 
Haines joined his church, which met at Southwater, near 
Horsham. To the end of his life he clung to this fellowship, 
though none of his children seem to have followed him. He 
was a man of wealth and ability, farmer, inventor, manu- 
facturer, student of the poor law; though at first he and 
Caffin were close friends, his eminence and his influential 
London friends somewhat alienated Cafiin. When Haines 
after patient experiment discovered a means of cleaning 
trefoil or hop clover so as to improve the seed, and applied 
for a patent, Caffin dissuaded him, and attacked him in the 
church. In the autumn of 1672 he summoned Haines to 
discuss in the church-meeting whether patents were allowable 
to a Christian; he classed patentees, as covetous, with 
idolators and unclean persons, and threatened to excom- 
municate all concerned. At a second meeting Caffin con- 
demned patents as a cause of offence in the world, and a 



GLEANINGS, 1652-1680 xiii. 

cause of offence to the weak brethren. Haines was in- 
terrupted and insulted by Caffin when he tried to reply, so> 
he appealed. Thereupon Caffin excommunicated him, speci- 
fying as the reason that the greed of Haines was giving 
general scandal in the country, and reflecting on the church. 
Within three days he published this excommunication, and 
presently sent Haines a formal paper, specifying the grounds 
of this action. 

A law-suit on the matter, in which Caffin was not directly 
concerned, resulted in a speedy and thorough victory for 
Haines early in 1673, and a friend then tried to bring about 
a reconciliation within the church by a meeting in London 
at a Coffee house. Five prominent Particular Baptists, not 
of the Assembly, heard the case, admonished Caffin for daring 
to do such a thing when he had no word of God for it, and 
advised him to go home and reform the matter. By their 
initials we may suppose these " most eminent in London " 
were Gosnold of Paul's Alley, Watson of Coleman Street or 
Wise of East Smithfield, Perry of Coleman Street, Nehemiah 
Cox or William Collins of Petty France, and Samuel TuU 
of Petty France. The mediators being all Particular Baptists, 
the Horsham church merely said, " We do not own them." 

Haines hereupon lodged his appeal formally with the 
Assembly, notifying the church. The Assembly met in June, 
1673, when its chief concern was with the charge made by 
Thomas Monk against Caffin, of New Eutychianism, as tO' 
which Monk had published a book in the spring. Caffin 
retorted with a charge of fornication against Monk, as we 
hear from D'Assigny. And this cannot be explained away 
as the technical term for marrying out of Connexion, for 
Caffin disclaimed that phrase, and we learn that he was the 
draughtsman of the Agreement in 1668. The Assembly 
sided with Monk, but we do not know on which count of the 
cross-charges. Then came up the appeal of Haines, who 
asked that the matter should be dealt with in full Assembly.. 
CafSn countered with a proposal to appoint six assessors who- 
should sit with the Quarterly Association, before which the 
Horsham church and Haines should plead: Haines refused. 
Monk then proposed that a committee of six should settle- 
at once, but Cafifin objected time was lacking. This being 
over-ruled, Caffin raised the point of order that, except by 
consent, appeals could be heard by the Assembly only after 
a sister congregation and the County Association had. 



xiv. GLEANINGS, 1652-1680 

successively been invoked. The point v/as apparently not 
decided, and the Assembly adjourned, though the practice 
of successive appeals was well established. 

Haines lodged a second appeal to be heard in 1674, 
and when the Assembly met on 20 May in that year, the 
appeal was accepted as a matter of grace, and a decision 
was given. Haines at once expressed his dissatisfaction, 
though curiously neither pamphleteer cared to quote what 
the decision was. It could not have been final, for the 
matter came up year after year, and at one stage Caffin was 
adjudged wrong on his own showing, and patents were 
declared lawful. But Haines wished the excommunication 
reversed, and this was denied until he followed the usual 
course with a sister congregation and a Quarterly Meetings 
though they did suggest that it be referred to arbitrators. 
At length he adopted the normal procedure, and exactly as 
he foretold, the influence of Cafifin in Sussex was so great 
that Haines lost at both stages. He then appealed in due 
form, and brought his case after five years' delay properly 
before the Assembly. 

Haines mentions some prominent members who sup- 
ported Caffin : — Four Messengers : George Hammon (of Bid- 
denden and Cranbrook), Captain Morcock (of Chatham), 
Uridge of Kent, and William Marner of St. Martins-le-Grand ; 
with also Thomas Croucher, John Miller (of Minterne), Francis 
Stanley (of Ravensthorpe), and John Amory (of Wrington); 
one of whom was chairman. They opposed admitting the 
appeal, but it was heard. They managed, however, to 
prolong and confuse the meeting till it broke up in disorder, 
the members saying that " God had withdrawn Himself from 
them." 

This meeting apparently took place in 1679, for the next 
and final stage was on 3 June 1680. Haines had meanwhile 
printed a broadsheet which recapitulated all the previous 
history, and which is our main source for it, though much is 
to be gleaned from books on each side issued in 1674 ^^^ 
1675, two by Haines and two by Caffin. It wound up with 
the ultimatum that unless the Assembly would reverse Caffin's 
acts and would admonish his abettors, he would have recourse 
to a criminal prosecution. 

When the Assembly met, he put in a printed paper — 
perhaps this very broadside — not only claiming his own 
vindication, but making three charges against Caffin (i) for 



GLEANINGS, 1652-1680 xv. 

contemning the laws and prerogative of the king, (2) for 
excommunicating when no human law had been trans- 
gressed, (3) for maintaining principles and tenets and dis- 
cipline which were dangerous to the State, unjust and cruel 
to individuals. He also challenged all the members named 
above, demanding that they should not act as judges. In 
all this, Haines was strictly following Scripture order, not to 
go to law against a believer before unbelievers; but it was 
clear that if he remained excommunicated, this injunction 
did not apply to him, and he would manifestly be free to 
indict Caffin before the jiidges of the land. 

Our interest will be largely on the organization and 
theories revealed in one paragraph : — " And now possibly with 
a pitiful Jugling Hypocrisie you may exclaim against the 
thing, viz. : Superintendency and Dependency, which you 
owned when I entered my appeal, but now you really pretend 
the Churches of God are Independent: well, then, what are 
you a General Assembly for? Are you nothing but Ropes 
of Sand, and are you not gross Hypocrites and Juglers not 
to tell me so before ? " He presses the alternative, either 
decide the appeal, or dissolve the Assembly and own they 
are Independents like the Particular Baptists : " they meddle 
not with State Affairs; they assume not to excommunicate 
Persons, but upon the same terms as they baptize them, viz., 
plain text of Scripture for their warrant therein." 

His cause was heard and decided. The Assembly 
reversed the excommunication, and ordered Caffin to rescind 
it, which he promised to do. By 7 June, 1680, Haines 
penned an appendix to his Appeal, and reprinted it as his 
triumphant vindication. As he died in May of 1685, we 
see how the extant Minutes are silent on this episode. But 
it is conceivable that some day we may discover the records, 
and if it was possible to meet even in 1674 ^^^ 1675, we 
may now believe that the meetings were practically annual: 
indeed it appears that the Minutes for 1683 and 1685 were 
offered for sale within the last twenty years. 

From Haines' Appeal we can add at the top of page xxxiv. 
in our first volume the following who were Messengers : — 
1679 George Hammon of Biddenden and Cranbrook. 
1679 Captain Edward Morecock of Chatham.f 1693. 
1679 Uridge of Kent.

of Ravensthorpe was not yet a Messenger at this date.

C.1710 Nathanael Locking of Wisbech.f 1725. 

From the Fen Stanton Records at page 1 57 we may add 
to the List of Leaders before 1661, Israel Cave and Edmond 
Smith, Elders of Wisbech. 

For the period of persecution Crosby supplies a few 
names in Northamptonshire, at III., 128: — William Bliss of 
Weston by Weedon, Robert Bringhurst, John Gilby of Long 
Buckby, John Grunden of Cocket near Towcester, John Jeas 
of East Haddon, Joseph Slater, William Smith of Weston and 
Welton, John Staughton of Blisworth. Wood tells us that 
Stephen Curtis of Harringworth was buried on 5 February, 
1727; William Stanger of Harringworth, carpenter, farmer 
and minister is known by his descendant's memoir; Ben- 
jamin Morley of Ravensthorpe was alive in 1672. 

From the reports to the bishop of London in 1669 we 
learn that Samuel Loveday was a coat-seller, of Aldgate 
parish, Whitsun Yard, East Smithfield. The State Papers 
show that Thomas Lamb was in trouble with the High Com- 
mission as early as 1639, when he lived at Colchester; next 
year he was sent to the Fleet prison, whence he was released 
on 25 June with the condition that he neither preached nor 
baptized nor attended conventicles.

Reunited upon the Terms hereinafter Expressed. 
London June loth. 1731. 

WHEREAS there has been a division in the General 
Assembly, of the people commonly called General Baptists, 
which usually met in this City :^ The said General Assembly 

' The division of 1696, temporarily healed in 1704, had soon been repeated, 
as the preceding minutes show. As the focus of the other party was in Bucks, 
with the Midland churches generally adhering, they usually held their meetings 
at Stony Stratford, and entitled them this time, General Assembly, not General 
Association, thus asserting at least their equal importance with this Assembly, 
if not even implying that the Bucks party was the real sole Assembly. But 
in 1730 the Stony Stratford Assembly asked leave to meet at White's Alley 
in Whitsun week of 1731. This manifestly led to the reunion here detailed. 
The old Assembly met this year at Glass-house yard, as appears by a letter 
in 1733 from Northants. 

I 



2 SECOND ASSEMBLY BOOK [1731 

being by the kind Providence of God once more met to- 
gether : HAVE after mature deliberation, agreed, that there 

1st. Art. of Union be an hearty Reunion, on the BeUef of the Doctrine of 
Universal Redemption, and the Belief and Practice of the 
Foundation Principles of the Doctrine of Christ mentioned 
Hebrews 6; i, 2, as they have been, and are believ'd and 

[Page 3.] practised, By the Churches *By the Churches of this Assembly. 

2nd. Artof Union And it is further agreed, that no preacher or Member of 

[expunged in the Churches, now, belonging to this Assembly, (or that 
may belong thereunto) shall Preach, Write or Urge, in dis- 
course, such Controversie about the Doctrine of the Holy 
Trinity, which shall be unto the Disturbance of the Churches 
Peace, and naturally hath tendency to cause Divisions and 
Offences Rom 16, 17. And if any person whatsoever shall 
not have due Regard hereunto, he shall be accounted dis- 
orderly, and according to the word of the Lord be proceeded 
against as such, in tlie Church, Association or Assembly, 
where the Offence is Committed; forasmuch as many of 
us, have declared our Sentiments concerning the said 
Doctrine, in the first and second Articles, drawn up the 10 
of June 1704, Still believing the Same, and do desire Peace 
with Purity in all the Churches of Christ. 

In Testimony whereof we have hereunder subscribed our 
names.

Agreed at this Assembly as follows 

1. That whereas it hath appeared to this Assembly that [Family worship 

^■^ , . . ^ and catechizing 

there is a very great Decay of Holiness & Piety in many of "f chudren.] 
the Members of the Baptized Churches and rising Generation 
wch is we esteem greatly occasioned by the want of Family 
Worship & a Pious & Religious Education of Children 
Tis therefore agreed by the Members and accordingly they 
do advise That each in the Ministry do choose to treat on 
such a Subject or Subjects on purpose to exhort all their 
Hearers to instruct and Chatechize their Children, tO' en- 
deavour to bring them up in the Nurture & Fear of the Lord 
& to acquaint them betimes wth the Doctrines and Principles 
of the Gospel, And likewise to choose another Subject or 
Subjects to exhort their Hearers to maintain Family Worship 
in Family & Secret Prayer & Praises to God & continually 
to visit & encourage their Members & others to the Practice 
of these good Duties and to enquire whether they put the 
same in Practice and that these things be recommended to 
to the rest of the Churches wch. are now absent. 

2. That every Church do endeavour to raise what Money [Funds for 
they can Annually in the best Method and send the same """'^ '^' 
by their Elder or Representatives to the Association with

Whereas this Assembly is Informd by some of ye 
Members, yt Several of our Sister Churches have Ob- 
jected against ye Second Article of our sd. Union last 
year, we do for ye Satisfaction of such Brethren & 
Churches, declare, yt we understand ye Sense of that 
Agreemt. in ye Manner following ; That Christian Charity 
& Love, as well as common Prudence, requires every 
Member not to disturb ye Society to wich. he belongs 
& especially in Things wch. naturally tend to cause 
Divisions & Offences; so that here is no Restraint laid 
on Conscience, but is universally binding if this 
Article had never been made: nor are ye Rights of 
any particular Person or Church in ye least Infring'd

upon by it, but rather defended & enlarged; & This

& brought wt. they had drawn up for that End & after

The Church of Christ in Northamptonshire, under ye 
Pastoral Care of Bro. Wm. Stanger, Bro. Jon. Shenston, & 
Bro. John Lea' met at an Associate meeting at Buckby Mill 
March 29: 1733 To the General Assembly of Messengers! 
Elders & Brethren Representatives of Several Churches in 
this Nation, owning the Doctrine of Universal Redemption, 
&c. meeting in London; 

Beloved Brethren, 

iWe reed, last Year at our Associate Meeting an 
Account of a Union being made between the Churches 
wch. were formerly divided: the Foundation of this 
Union not giving us full Satisfaction either through ye 
Weakness of our Understandings, or through some 
Obscurity in ye Thing it self, proved the Occasion why 
we had no Representatives wth you last Year: in our 
Association this Year ye same Matter coming again 
under our Consideration, & We not yet arriving at full 
Satisfaction, were moved from the Strong Desire we 
have of being in a firm & hearty Union wth all those 
who hold the same Faith, & teach ye same Doctrine wch. 
we our Selves believe, & are perswaded are true to send 
our beloved Brother Thos. Haile to you this Year as 
our Representative, by whom we hope to receive from 
you such farther Light as may, not only cause us to 
rejoyce our Selves in what is done, but also render 
us able to give a good Reason thereof to any who shall 
ask it of us. 

Beloved Brethren 

The Union being made without Signing Articles 
of Faith & Union, is one Main Thing that renders us not 

' This heading is remarkable. Stanger lived at North Moulton, Lea at 
Spratton ; the " Church," not " churches," is specified, yet it is acknowledged 
to be an Associate meeting. The phraseology suggests that the congregations 
were so closely knit, that they could speak of themselves as one Church, to 
which all the Elders belonged. It is a grave misfortune that in the subsequent 
decay of the churches, the Association records disappeared. It is well known 
that on the foundation of the churches. Particular Baptist churches arose in 
some cases. 



White's Alley] ASSEMBLY 15 

so well satisfied as we could wish, now seeing you 
declare you believe ye ist. & 2d. Articles drawn up 
June loth 1704 to be true wie wish you had also been 
willing to subscribe to them, because this would have 
removed all Scruples from us; & as for our Selves, we 
are perswaded, notwithstanding all that hath been ob- 
jected against it, that there is no evil in Subscribing 
what we *believe to be true, but is rather commendable, [Page 15.] 
when it preserves a good Understanding between 
Churches, & tends to maintain Peace & Unity. And 
on the other Hand, we don't think that the not Sub- 
scribing Articles 2(w.hen the refusing is not make a 
Cloak to Shelter Errors)^ a matter of so great Moment 
as ko cause Division amongst those who otherwise believe 
the same Things : and we hope therefore Beloved to 
receive Assurance from you that not only you who were 
present at your Meetinghouse at Glasshoiase Yard, & ["but only the 

^ ■' " ' men who 

were Parties conoem'd in making up this Union, are signed.] 
in the Belief of the aforesaid Articles, but also that ye 
Churches represented by you, are found in the same 
Faith; for wth all such Churches we desire a hearty 
Union : yet Brethren, we should be glad to know from 
wt. Reason you are moved to forbear Signing Articles 
of Faith wch you profess to believe ye Truth of; 
especially when the doing this will contribute to ye 
Satisfaction of your Brethren, & be a visible Demon- 
stration that we are of ye same Mind, & of the same 
Judgmt. 

One Thing more we beg leave humbly to offer to 
your Consideration namely this; There are some 
Churches among the General Baptists, that are fallen 
into ye way of Singing, Psalms of David, or other Mens ^^'^^^*^ 
Composures wth tuneable Notes, & a mixed Multitude; anging'] 
wch way of Singing appears to us wholly unwarrantable 
from the Word of God; Now Brethren, you as we are 
informed do admit of such to become as Members wth 
you; by wch., whether you approve of their Way of 
Singing, or whether you disapprove, & use any Means 
to bring them off from it, or whether you look on it as

*THE ANSWER 

The General Assembly of Messengers, Elders & Brethren, 
Representatives of the Several Churches of Baptized Believers, 
constituted according to Heb. 6. 1,2, and owning ye Doctrine 
of Universal Redemption now met at Whites Alley May i6- 
17: 1733- send Christian Salutation to our beloved Brethren 
Wm. Stanger, John Shenston & Bro.^ John Lee, & ye rest 
of the Brethren who hold an Association wth. them at 
Buckby Mill in Northamptonshire. 

Christian & Beloved Brethren, 

We reed, read & considered your Letter by our 
Brother Haile, containing the Reasons why we have 
no other Representatives from you at this Time, & 
desiring *to know from wt. Reason we are moved to 
forbear Signing Articles of Faith.* 

As to the first concerning the Obscurity* of ye 
Articles upon wch. we imited; we had great Reason 
to hope that our Bro. Stanger, who was present in that 
Assembly, & last Year sent us a Satisfactory Letter 
about ye Union then made, might have given you full 
Light into the Sense of this Assembly: But as we find 
you are yet in doubt & yt the Expedient wch. we drew 
up last Year to explain those Articles, did not come to

your Hands, we give you the same at large as follows, 
which we hope will be a full Satisfaction to you. (See 
ye 6: Article 1732) 

As to your Request that *you should be glad to 
know from wt. Reason we are moved to forbear Signing 
Articles of Faith> It is because we find neither Precept sign Articles] 
nor Precedent for our so doing in ye Sacred Scriptures 
of Truth; wch. we believe to be the only Rule both 
of Faith & Practice, in all Religious Services : & we 
believe that our dear Lord & Saviour Jesus Christ & 
his holy Apostles, who were endowed wth. an infalliable they are faiubie] 
Spirit, had the only Power & Right to prescribe Laws 
&' Rules, & appoint the Bounderies of his Churches 
Obedience, Communion & Union & whoever shall attempt 
to do such a Thing now by pretending to explain some 
Scriptures, is usurping ye Authority of Christ & his 
Apostles, without they are assured that they *can in- 
falliably determine ye Sense of Scripture, & can give 
a Proof of their Infalliablity, wch. we presume no 
Protestant Christian will pretend to, & therefore every 
sincere Christian must be left to judge for himself wt. 
ye Sense of Scripture is, according to the Light wch. 
God giveth him, Phil. 3. 15, 16. Therefore we esteem 
it unlawful for any Man to make & impose humane 
Compositions as a Test or Boundery of any Christians 
Faith for because they thereby offer ye highest Affront 
to the infalliable Spirit of ye Eternal God wch. inspired 
the holy Penmen of ye Old & New Testaments wch. are 
the only Rules tO' prove & try every Mans Work, both 
of Faith & Practice by. For whatever is not according 
to yt Rule, is as represented in Gal. i. 6, 7, 8. And

HYMNODY 21 

Since the question of sinking was mooted last in 1689, there had been 
developments. Benjamin Keach, who was responsible for the innovation, 
published in 1691 nearly three hundred hymns, entitled " Scriptural Melody." 
His chief disciple was Joseph Stennett, senior, who since 1689 was pastor of 
the Sabbatarian Calvinistic Baptist Church, worshipping on Saturdays at 
Devonshire Square, and subsequently at Pinner's Hall. Stennett in 1697 
published hymns for use at the Lord's Supper, which grew to the number of 
fifty. In 1712 he broke new ground with twelve hymns for use at baptisms — 
other verses for special occasions were also issued, and his Sabbath hymn, 
" Another six days' work is done," is often sung still upon the Lord's Day. 

These two men inspired a few others. Bishop Ken wrote one or two 
hymns which made their way beyond his Nonjuring Church. A young 
Independent minister in London, named Isaac Watts, wrote a few hymns, 
into one of which he " conveyed " several verses of Stennett's. He published 
a little volume in 1707, entitled " Hymns and Spiritual Songs." Twelve years 
later, he struck out a new line by "The Psalms of David, Imitated in the 
Language of the New Testament," followed in 1720 by " Divine and Moral 
Songs for the use of Children." Far away in the Yorkshire dales, a Calvinistic 
Baptist minister, named Alverey Jackson, wrote hymns for the Lord's Supper^ 
and then ventured to sum up the chief points of his lengthy sermons in verses 
which he lined out for the congregation to sing. 

By two different channels these influences told on the General Baptists. 
Joseph Stennett was indeed a Sabbatarian and a Calvinist, but he was ready 
to preach for other Baptist churches, and his great ability — recognised by the 
courts of William and Anne — led to frequent invitations. Till 1700, he 
regularly conducted the worship at Paul's Alley, which was in close touch with 
High Hall, Hart Street, and White's Alley. Curiously enough, Paul's Alley 
became the home of a Particular Baptist church from Virginia Street, which 
gave up its premises to the Goodman's Fields church in 1712. 

Not far from Southampton, Watts' birthplace, in the " sweet fields beyond 
the swelling flood," was the city of Chichester, where the Baptist church was 
tended by Richard Drinkwater and Matthew Randall. The latter was invited 
in 1722 to take charge of the Virginia Street General Baptist church, and many 
changes ensued. A special visitation of the whole church was undertaken on 
the point whether singing of hymns were agreeable. After trial of one hymn 
during the services, a further step was taken in 1729 by closing after the 
sermon with a second. These minutes show that the members upheld their 
practice on scriptural grounds, that they failed to convince others, and that they 
were even disapproved of. The Assembly churches long remained songless, 
and when in 1782 the New Connexion decided to have simple congregational 
singing without musical instruments, Gilbert Boyce soon published a volume 
against the practice. 

To complete the subject, in advance of time, it may be added that, in 
1803, Mrs. Alice Flowerdew, a member of the Glass-House Yard church, then 
housed at Worship Street, published some " Poems, on moral and religious 
subjects," reprinted next year, and augmented in 181 1. Also in 1805, John 
Dobell issued a collection of " Hymns and Spiritual Songs," and followed it 
next year with "A New Selection of . . Evangelical Hymns," many 
original, which ran to three editions. But otherwise the lack of interest in 
song among the General Baptists was as marked as among the sister com- 
munity, the Society of Friends.

' A later pencil note marks this column, Reps by J. Morriss. Really

has the obsolescent sense, " except." 

'" A later pencil note marks the first three signatures. Mess. This is 
correct. Obviously the signing began here, in the right column, Messengers 
first, then Elders, then Representatives ; and when the foot of the page was 
reached, the representatives continued in a left column.

Order'd, That Bro. Randall, Ingram, Morris & Smith, 
do acquaint Bro. MuUener and the Church he stands related 
to, & the Church in the Park, that 'twas their Omission that 
they were not invited to joyn with the Assembly at this Time,, 
and are now order'd to invite them to the next Assembly. 

Bro. Mercer and Drinkwater have given Account that 
they have treated with Bro. Hobbs and Friends at Canterbury,, 
but can't as yet prevail with them, as concerning his taking- 
upon him the Officer of a Messenger. 

*Northamptonshire Letter being read & consider'd, it 
was order'd that a Committee be appointed further to con- 
sider of the same, and accordingly Bro. Miller, Welton, 
Killingworth and Johnson were order'd to draw up an Answer 
to the said Letter. 

The Committee return'd and the Letter by them drawa 
up being Several Times read, and approv'd by the Assembly- 
it was order'd that the said Letter be transcribed in the- 
Assembly Book, and that their Letter be kept in the Box. 

And whereas the Second Article of our Union Jun. 10. 
1 73 1, was incerted wth. no other view than to Satisfie our 
Brethren in & about Buckinghamshire & Northamptonshire,, 
but it not giving them clear Satisfaction, & furthermore they- 
having desired the aforesaid Article to be expung'd, we 
have agreed thereunto, and accordingly do expunge the 
same. 



The Letter order'd to be wrote in Answer to the North- 
amptonshire Letter, is as follows :

1735- 



From the General Assembly met at London May 28, 29,. 

To the Northamptonshire Association: 
Beloved Brethren 

We hoped that what we heretofore wrote to you„ 
would have removed all Uneasiness from you, but as; 
you yet desire further Satisfaction, & that we would, 
give you our Sentiments of the Doctrine of the Trinity,, 
either in Scripture or other Words ; To shew you there- 
fore, that we have no ambiguous Meaning in what we- 
wrote to you, & that it was not from a disbelief of the 
Doctrine of the Trinity as contain'd in the Scriptures, 
that -we decline Signing of Articles, but to^ prevent 
humane Explanations being imposed upon us, as if of 



Goswell Street] ASSEMBLY 33 

Divine Authority, we hereby assure you ithat we firmly 
beUeve in the Doctrine of the Trinity, as contained in 
the Scriptures, even in ye Father Son, and Holy Spirit ;i 
that there are ^Three that bear Record in Heaven, the 
Father, the Word, and the Holy Ghost ; and these Three 
are One.^ And, as the Second Article of our Union ["ana expunge 

the Second 

which was niade designedly to give you & our Brethren Article to piease 
in and about Buckinghamshire Satisfaction, & to prevent 
any Doctrines being propagated that might give you 
Offence, doth not appear to be to your Satisfaction, we 
are willing at your & their Desire, wholly to expunge 
the Same : wch. is accordingly done by this Assembly. 

*As to the Second Thing you seem to want Satis- [Page 35.] 
faction in, namely the permitting those that practise [" but decline t* 

. ' ■* .,° ,_. , expel thosewho 

Smgmg of Psalms wth. musical Notes and Conjoynea s>ng jointly] 
Voices in the Worship of God, to be Members of our 
Assembly, we must in Part, at Least, refer you tO' what 
we said in our last on that Subject; we then, & yet 
believing that in so doing wie act agreeable to the Con- 
stitution of ye Assembly, We being ye Representatives of 
ye Baptized Churches owning the Doctrine of Universal 
Redemption, & the Six Principles of Christ, mentioned 
Heb. 6. I, 2. all which (as far as we know) are believed 
and practised by all the Churches that are in Union 
with '.us : and therefore, tho' some very few of us practise ['though most 

„.. . -. ,.„ , •^ . ^ , sing otherwise J 

Singing in a Manner different from us in general, we 
don't apprehend that to be a Sufficient Reason to exclude 
them from being Members of the Assembly, there being 
in our Esteem a great Difference betwixt joyning in 
Union & Communion with them (as you express it) 
& permitting them to joyn with us in a General Assembly. 
It can't reasonably be expected that in this Assembly 
we should enter into a Dispute about the Manner of 
Singing in the Service of God, it is plain to us, that 
Singing of Psalms is frequently mentioned in the New 
Testament, and the Apostle James expresly says, ^If 
any be merry, let him sing Psalms j^ the Difference is not 
about Singing, but the Manner of doing it, whether with 
musical Notes and conjoyned Voices, or otherwise; we 
could heartily wish & desire we were all of one Mind 
in this as well as all other Things; but as the Weak-

t'' for our basis

ness of human Understanding is such, that Things appear 
in different Lights to different Persons such a Concord 
is rather to be desired than expected in this World : We 
therefore, are willing not to impose our Opinion & 
Practice upon others in Things of this Nature, nor 
refuse admitting them to our Assembly because they 
practise a Mode of Worship, which from Scripture doth 
not appear clear to us all; especially considering that 
they profess to believe and practise those Things which 
are the Basis and Foundation on wch. this Assembly 
unites. 

So Brethren, we commend you to God, & to the 
Word of his Grace, and Subscribe our Selves your 
Brethren in the Lord, in the Name and Behalf of the 
whole :

*Having consider'd the Letter from the Lincoln Associa- 
tion signifying their joyning with this Assembly an Answer 
was wrote in Answer to the same. 

Whereas there hath been Letters sent us from Great 
Yarmouth, Wendover & Harringworth representing to us 
the Necessity they are under of building Meeting houses 
at their several Places, the Want they are in of Money to 
build ye same We recommend to them, (and all our Friends 
in the like Circumstances) that they either by Messengers, 
appointed^ for that Purpose or by Letters apply to such 
Persons and Churches within their reach, to desire their 
assistance in so good a Work, and we recommend the Case 
of our Brethren at Great Yarmouth, Wendover, & Harring- 
worth to the Consideration of our Brethren, & all good and 
well disposed Persons, as worthy of the same, and as what 
we approve. 

Order'd that Thanks be given to Bro. Morris, & that

of the Assembly, wch. was done accordingly by Bro. Mercer,

Agreed that our Bror. Mercer, or Drinkwater do write 
a Letter to the Church at Smarden in Kent to invite that 
Church to joyn with this Assembly the next year. 

Agreed that all the Ministers make it their care to 
Affect the Minds of the People (to whom they may stand 
related) with the want of Ministers and Spiritual Gifts, and 
to stir up and press them to set apart some time or Times 
by Fasting and Prayer to the Lord from increase of Spiritual 
Gifts and that great care be taken to encourage and instruct 
Persons in so good a work, and to appoint Seasonable Times 
weekly or Monthly for exercise and Improvemt. and to use 
all other proper Measures for Supporting and raising* a 
Ministry in the Church.

to represent the State of the Society for relieving poor 

^ i 

^ This trust had been founded in 1698, by a merchant captain from Penzance, 
now resident in Mile End, and a member of White's Alley, in whose books he 
figures occasionally. He appointed two trustees from each of the five chief 
London churches to hold two Essex farms, and after some temporary annuities, 
to pay £i to each of these churches, £,<, each to Dunning's Alley and Norwich, 
£,\<, for travelling expenses of the Messengers, and then to divide the balance 
among the ministers and poor of their churches. The trustees had some 
trouble with two members of White's Alley in 1726, settled by elaborate 
arbitration, and early in 1727 a special meeting was called of the five churches. 
This was important in one respect, other than that of property, for the trustees 
offered a definition of the word " General," that it was intended to express the 
doctrine of Universal Redemption, and the definition was accepted. This 
repudiated the meaning iaffixed in 1697 at the " General " Association, and 
reverted to the original significance. 

* The word " and " follows, but is crossed out. 

' Corrected from " formally."

Ministers and to exhort them to make yearly Collections 
for the same purposes and that they either do it by writing 
or printing as to them shall seem meet; and also that they 
press the Severall Churches to make good their places in 
future Assemblies.

• The name " Hornchurch " was written, then crossed out, and the new 
name Upminster was written below. The two places are close together, but 
the new name appears henceforward. Perhaps there was no meeting-house 
.at either place, but members' houses were used, and most now lived in 
Upminster. One of Pierce Johns' farms lay close to these villages, and very 
Jikely the tenant was a supporter of the General Baptist cause. Similarly 
Mersea Island lay between Colchester and the other farm.

THE GENERAL BAPTIST FUND. 

The following letter is apparently a circular sent out to many churches 
this year. It gives in very lucid form the objects of the General Baptist Fund, 
which henceforth is patronized by the Assembly. In 1717 a Particular Baptist 
Fund was formed, which two years later declined to admit Paul's Alley church 
as a subscriber. The ministers there founded this fund in 1725 on a wider 
doctrinal basis, and Crosby hoped that this would attract the wider support.

But as in 1738 the younger fund became so closely connected with the 
Assembly, the Calvinists never gave it any substantial help. This letter is 
copied from page 69 in the church-book of Bradburn and Sevenoaks, now in 
the British Museum, Addl. MS. 36,709. A long final paragraph, chiefly a 
cento of texts, has been omitted. 

To the Church of Christ at Tunbridge Wells, 
under the Pastoral Care of our well-beloved 
Brethren Thomas Benge and William Ashdown 

Beloved Brethren, 

There having been several consultations in ye 
General Assembly for some Years past about raising 
Money in ye Churches towards better supporting ye 
Ministry & encouraging Spiritual Gifts; and it being 
considered yt there is a Society in this City wch has 
Succesfully carried on this good Work several Years; 
it was thereupon unanimously agreed, " That Collections 
" should be made yearly by ye several Churches belong- 
" ing to this Assembly, & be remitted to Mr. Wm : Steed 
" on Little Tower Hill, to be applied by ye Society of 
" wch he is Treasurer, for ye Relief of poor Ministers, 
" & for the Improvement of Gifted Brethren." 

It will not be doubted but this Society is conveniently 
situated to hold a Correspondence wth ye Churches in 
every part of ye Kingdom, & can therefore easily receive 
ye Collections from thence, & distribute ym where there 
is occasion. But for your more entire Satisfaction it 
was order'd, That a particular Acct. of their Design 
& manner of Proceeding should be drawn up & sent 
to the Several Churches : In pursuance of wch Order 
we now send it you, not doubting but you will approve 
the Design of ye Society, & strengthen ye Hands of ye 
Managers by your hearty Concurrence. 

The Society consists of ye Elders of Six Con- 
gregations & as many Messengers as each of them has 
a right to send. A Congregation wch contributed £20 
at first & collects £5. yearly, has a right to send one 
Messenger. A Congregation wch contributed £40, & 
collects £10. yearly, sends two Messengers; If a Church 
raised £60. & brings yearly £15. it has a right to send 
three Messengers; but no Church may send more than 
three. These Messengers or Managers from ye Churches 
are chosen every Year. But if any Person gives to the 
Society £50. being a Baptist, he has a right tO' be a 



GENERAL BAPTIST FUND 45 

Manager as long as he lives; & he who- gives £5. is 
qualified to be a Manager for one Year, & so long as 
he continues to give Five Pounds a year. 

The Society has agreed to meet Statedly on ye First ["monthly 
Monday in every Month, & Seven Managers, Five of ihem 
not being Ministers, make a Board to do Business, & 
their Proceedings are entered in a Minute Book. Every 
one bears his own Expence. 

The Yearly Collections of ye Churches are appointed c Annual

Persons in ye Countrey, should be remitted to Mr. Steed 
ye Treasurer, or to Some *Some other Member of the [69 verso.]

One half of ye yearly Dividend is distributed among ["half to poor 
poor Ministers (chiefly in the Countrey) who are Elders, 
or Stated Preachers. Out of those Ministers who are 
recommended tO' the Society, the most necessitous are 
chosen as near as can be judged. If any Minister cannot 
be taken in at the first Recommendation, his name is 
minuted down, in order to be admitted — when there is 
a Vacancy, or the Society can enlarge the Dividend. 
We judged it proper to mention this, yt none of our 
Friends in ye Countrey may take it amiss if the Minister 
by them recommended is not taken in at the first, since

next Opportunity.

^ " The other Churches in Town " specifies nothing as to doctrine. Since 
the reunion nothing had been said about the Confession of 1660-1663, whether 
in the original form, or in Grantham's enlargement, or in Hooke's revision. 
The churches were shrinking in number and in size. High Hall had migrated 
in 1717 to Park, and its Elder, John Jenkins the ribbon-weaver, had now ceased 
work, if indeed he were not dead. Dunning's Alley had split, and in 1729 
Pierce Johns' trustees voted that it was extinct. This very year Hart Street 
dissolved, and its Elder James Smith united with White's Alley as a private 
member. Mill Yard had had a curious experience, for about 1721 the 
Calvinistic Sabbatarian Baptists, once under Joseph Stennett, had joined these 
General Sabbatarian Baptists ; but when Robert Cornwaite became Elder in 
1726, the Calvinists drew off, objecting to his Socinian views. The Joseph 
Davis Charity sufficed however to keep it alive. There were also two other 
churches which had once been distinctly Calvinistic, but were less sure of their 
ground, (i) Gosnold's church at Paul's Alley in the Barbican, served by 
Thomas Plant, Joseph Stennett and Richard Allen, had in 1700 taken leave of 
Stennett expressly because he was a Calvinist; though Allen joined the 
Calvinistic Association in 1705, two churches at once withdrew from it. The 
Calvinist element was strengthened in 1712 by the adhesion of Humphrey 
Burroughs and 24 other members from the dissolved church at Pennington 
Street (Virginia Street), so that by 1717 the church put up splendid new 
premises with a baptistery, largely at the expense of the Hollis family, it had 
220 members, and was rich in ministers, Nathanael Gale and Joseph Burroughs 
being chief. Yet in 1719 the Particular Baptist Fund declined to admit the 
church to membership. Five years later, an Independent minister from Trow- 
bridge, James Foster, was baptized and associated with Burroughs in the 
pastorate, and Isaac Kimber from the General Baptist church at Nantwich 
soon became assistant. An outsider classed all three ministers in 1731 as 
Socinian, but they were distinctly Baptist, and objected to mixed communion. 
A generation was yet to elapse before this church responded in any degree to 
overtures from the Assembly, though it was on good terms with White's Alley. 
(2) The great original Particular Baptist church of 1633 had subdivided 
extensively; two of the branches reunited in 1707 at Artillery Lane, Spital- 
fields, deliberately sanctioning hymns at the service. Nathanael Hodges was

the minister then, and the church was expressly invited to join the Particular 
Baptist Fund in 1717, though two years later he declined to sign a Trinitarian 
declaration. When he resigned, being rich and a knight. Dr. Kinch from 
Paul's Alley followed in 1721, and he did not join the Coffee-house Society of 
Calvinistic Baptist ministers. Ten years later he was succeeded by John 
Weatherley from Taunton, and under him the character of the church 
insensibly altered. Burroughs and Kimber being close friends of Weatherley. 
About this time the congregation shifted to Pinners' Hall, using it in the 
afternoon only. But not till 1754 were the links with the General Baptists 
tightened by calling Richard Barron from Deptford, and not till 1771 did the 
minister actually join the Assembly. These facts show the increasing 
difficulty as to ministers ; again and again the country was drained of its 
best men to serve the town, and the gallant attempts of White's Alley 
to foster a college in London, repeated in 1725, came to nothing. The 
few ministers served two or three churches apiece as the older men died 
off, while the churches died or fused or shared the same building. It was 
not yet understood that while the practice of Believers' Baptism formed a 
certain bond between the General Baptists and the Particulars, yet the gulf 
was very real and very deep. Thomas Crosby was doing his best to confuse 
together the two groups, greatly to the misleading of subsequent generations- 
When two baptisteries were built in 1717, each group had subscribed to each 
building ; and Benjamin Stinton had done his best to unite the ministers in a 
Society meeting monthly at a Coffee-house. But the Particular Baptists 
persisted in holding aloof, founding a Fund limited to Calvinists, and in 1724 a 
new Coffee-house Society which soon drew off all their ministers from Stinton's. 
Thus in 1730 this original — the British — reorganized, and though there never 
was any rule to exclude any for doctrinal reasons, and invitations were sent to 
Rhudd Brine and Dew, yet in practice it was even at that date a fraternal for 
the General Baptist ministers only. While however the two parties were 
blaming one another for Antinomianism and Socinianism, religion was at a 
very low ebb. Whitefield had begun preaching in 1736, John Wesley was to 
be drawn into the work in 1739, but his fervent Arminianism failed to revitalize 
the Confession of 1660, his ardent evangelism failed to excite the Messengers 
to renewed itinerancy, his genius for organization left the Assembly untouched.

' Abraham Mulliner died this year, after being Elder since 1703. The 
church dropped out of touch with the Assembly till 1772, under the following 
circumstances. John Ash worth was invited to the pastorate in November 1739 
(the summary in the church book written last century confounds him with his 
brother Thomas). John Ashworth was son of Richard Ashworth, a Calvinistic 
Baptist minister at Cloughfold in Lancashire, who had gone to help the General 
Baptist church at Nantwich, which was seeking fellowship rather on geographi- 
cal than on doctrinal affinities. He declined to come to White's Alley if it 
insisted that Laying on of hands was necessary after baptism. To secure him 
they waived the Fourth Principle, and therefore of course forfeited their place 
in this Assembly. He was ordained on 3 July, 1740 by Fry, Foster, Burroughs 
and Weatherley, a set of names that shows the White's Alley, Horslydown, 
Paul's Alley, and Artillery Lane churches already in close touch. The trustees 
of Pierce Johns' charity were offended, and apparently stopped supplies for the 
time. Ashworth however died between April and October 1742, and after the 
services had been conducted by Richard Barron and Charles Bulkley, the 
latter was installed as pastor on 4 April 1743, being then only 24 years old. 
Next year the church arranged with Paul's Alley for partial use of those 
premises, as the lease of White's Alley from the Merchant Tailors was soon 
expiring, and a fine of ;f35o was to be expected if renewal were desired. From 
this time the church apparently sank into inactivity for fifty years.

' The churches in East Kent were in great local trouble. A quarrel at 
Ramsgate in the Isle of Thanett resulted in a special meeting being held on 
14th July 1738, when John Hobbs of Canterbury, Robert Pyall of Dover, John 
Kingsford of Wingham and Thomas Benge a minister of Canterbury vainly 
tried to make peace. They were appealed from, Drinkwater and Mercer being 
invoked, and it was 1743 before the upholder of ancient standards was expelled 
as a scandal. Meanwhile the Elders were dying, and ministers were not 
coming forward. Eythorn and Sandwich were destitute, and Ramsgate seems 
to have borne the burden of all.

Commonwealth, probably owing to the labours of Thoma.s Collier, it adopted 
Calvinistic views, and held them till 1714. In this year James Sampson was 
invited from Exeter, which was the headquarters of the new Socinian movement, 
and unrest developed in the church, culminating in 1728 by his resignation on 
advice. For nine years he preached in the suburb of West Exe, and of course 
gathered a new church including many of his former hearers ; it was in this 
phase that he appeared at the Assembly in 1731. He had now come to 
London. Apparently there was trouble about the manse; in 1728 the 
Calvinists had acquired it from the owner, a colonist in Maryland, and by 1732 
they had remodelled the property, erecting a handsome meeting house at a 
cost of £328, and ordaining a new pastor from Moretonhampstead next year.

It will be seen that in the fifty years since 1689, the General Baptists had 
made no progress in London ; one church had been founded, but was just 
expiring- ; another had died out ; their places would soon be supplied by two 
ancient churches joining the Assembly, but this was no real increase.]

' No representative from Leicester. This year there began a movement 
at Ratby and Normanton which in fourteen years became Baptist, and by 
1770 had completely distanced the Leicester Association, becoming the nucleus 
of the New Connexion.

R. 



' Mill yard, as shown by Maitland's list, had long housed a Baptist 
church. This was a Seventh-day congregation, and therefore the premises 
were available on Sunday for another church. The Virginia street lease 
expired in 1741 at Lady-day, and the homeless church arranged to pay ;£io a 
year for the use of Mill yard on Sundays. The system of leasing, involving a 
large sum for the privilege of renewal, besides the annual rent, is responsible 
for the extinction of many Baptist churches which made no sinking fund or 
accumulated nothing against the expiry of leases.

as to Bro. Maulden, his 111 State of Health was thought to be 
a reasonable Excuse: As to Bro. Gyles and Bro. Bush they 
were also treated with, who after a considerable Time gave 
us room to hope that when some few Difficulties were removed 
they would pursuant to the Call of the Assembly submit 
to Ordination. 

Order'd that Bro: Drinkwater do visit the Church ia

^ The scribe reverts to the old practice of entering associations and 
churches which might be deemed constituents, even though not represented 
for years. No single scribe ever names explicitly the churches forming the 
Western or Northants or Leicester or Lincoln Association ; from these pages 
and from other sources some can be identified, but as the minute-books of the 
associations are not extant, their local history is not recovered as yet.

May 16 1744. 

To the Church of Xt Meeting on Bessels 
Green.^ Salutation. 

Beloved in the Lord. 

Your case not having been referred to any Sister 
Churches, nor to any Association, could not come 
regularly Tjefore the Assembly.' 

I was, at the same time, desired to sattisfie you, 
that was the cause of its ommission, and not any want 
of tender respect or Care for your good estate; and 
also to give you my private thoughts on the affair 
mentioned in your Letter, which I desire to do with 
equal humility and Freedom. It is agreed on all hands, 
that Mix'd Marriages are not only inexpedient but 
dangerous, and some times lead to very bad conse- 
quences, and, as Such, are to be prevented as much as 
miay be, by all seasonable advice, watchfulness and 
Caution. The only difficulty is; how the Church ought 
to deal with those Members who, after all, marry out 
of our Fellowship ? And here, I must acknowledg there 
may be circumstances full of aggravation; as when, for

marriages 

dangerous.]

time, those vfho, on all other accounts, are equally, if 
not more deservinsr. But to make it a sreneral rule, to [Each case to be

suspend from Comunion all, without exception, is what merits.] 

1 could never find defensible by the Word of God, or

The two most pertinent places of Scripture usually i. 

1 . i_ . rrn [Scripture is not 

brought to that end, seem to me, very insuincient. The clear,] 
Caution, " not to be unequally yoked together with un- 
believers &c."8 apparently relates to the Corinthians 
joining with the Pagans at their religious feasts and in 
their Idolatrous rites. This is plain, by only comparing 

2 Cor 6. 14, with I Epis 6. 10. Chap. 10. 16 ult. " Only 
in the Lord,"^ i Cor. 7. 39. can mean no more than, 
in the profession of Xtianity, in opposition to the pagan 
unbelievers. Because 

Tho there were then sects and divisions among the 2. 

ir- , ■ -I- •^- ri-i,-- [Sects of

divided as to communicate in parties : Some denyed 
the resurrection and overthrew ye Faith. Many Xtians 
Practicd the Legal rites in conjunction with those of 
the Gospel, So as that Xt would profit them nothing 
Gal 5. 2, while others as strenuously asserted their 
Freedom from the Mosaic yoak of Bondage: — 
*So likewise the Jews, under the law, they were divided [Page 349.] 
into parties, as opposite to each other as those of the 
Xitians. Now; tho, under both dispensations, believers 
are forbid to marry with unbelievers, yet I conceive 
'twill be hard to find the least intimation in ye Scriptures, 
that either the Jews then, might not marry into their 
diff'erent Sorts; or that the Xitians suite, who continued 
in the apostles doctrine and fellowship, are injoined, 
not to marry with those other Xitians who had, in some 
material points, departed from the symplicity of the 
Gospel. These cases, I think are nearly the same with 
ours, at present, and seem to mitigate the hard thoughts 
w;e have been apt to entertain of those who have Trans- 
gres'd in the affair under consideration. And if, at 
last, there should appear no direct prohibition of Xitians 
on their first establishment, from marrying with pious

Jews who believed in the true God but had not as yet 
embrac'd the Gospel; then I think your controversial, 
is entirely at an end. And then 

It ideserves to be consider 'd, whether suspension, 
from Comunion for so marrying be, in point of prudence, 
a proper method to reclaim offenders, or promote the 
Baptist Interest ? There are instances, not a few, of 
persons who, on account of such Severity,^ have never 
return'd to comunion, or Sought after it; while meek 
instructions, friendship and familiarity, have often 
brought over, even, ye adverse party to baptism and. 
coinunion with the Church. And there are many Candi- 
dates kept thereby from Joining with such Churches,, 
till they see how providence shall dispose of them.. 
For, in truth 

The severity contended for, especially as to some, 
persons, is a very great hardship. Marriage has its. 
rise and expedients from our nature and constitution. 
'Tis Honourable in all: 'Tis better to marry than to be- 
in pain. Now, since among us in this nation the women 
are not permitted to Look out for themselves ; and when, 
they have no offers from among the men of their own 
Comunity, what must they do? Mjust they, on pain of 
Excomunication, refuse every sober, virtuous. Christian- 
like person merely because he has not happened to be 
baptized by immersion or profession of faith? Is this; 
Consistent Vith Xitian charity and forbearance ? Yea, as 
to both sexes, 'tis scarcely possible, sometimes, not to- 
transgress: The accidental Sight, or Conversation of 
Strangers; their agreeable mein, complexion, or deport- 
ment, often Create such mutual likeing, and affection, 
as ^hall render marriage almost necessary and unavoid- 
able. "Where this likeing and affection are not the motives- 
to it, the true ends of marriage are seldom answr'd ; the 
persons are rarely happy. As therefore, the case and. 
circumstances, *the opportimities and inducements, are 
so Very different; time and chants happening to all 
persons, so as to render them unable to Foredetermine: 
themselves; I think the case of marriage cannot be 
brought linder any one Strict invariable rule by the 
Xitian church; No, Her censures are seldom useful,, 
never commendable, and often hurtful; while kindly

instruction, 'due Caution, friendly admonition, and prayer 
to God, seem to me the most likely means to prevent 
or regulate, all wrong proceedings in this most important 
part of life. 

Thus, Dear Brethren, I have given you my present 
thoughts in this case, and Submit them to your serious 
consideration. 

Remaining 

Your Brother in ye Gospel 

MiAT. RANDALL

have much greater influence on your determination than 
any thing I can say, I have therefore transcribed & 
sent it inclos'd.

In Consequence of the second article of last Years 
Agreemt We are assured now that our Brethren Richd. 
Chilton, Robt. Pyall and Matthias Copper were ordained to 
the Messengers Office by Bro. Benj. Miller on June 22, 1744, 
at Down ton Wilts. 

*A Question was put. Whether any Church may send 
[Two delegates more than Two Representatives to the Assembly, & it was 
carried in the Negative. 
3. In Consequence of the Third Article of our last Assembly, 

elected.] the Church at Webstone have recommended Bro. John Geere 

to the Messengers Office, which Recommendation was taken 
into Consideration & his Case & Character being generally 
known & approved by this Assembly, it is agreed by this 
Assembly that our said Bro. John Geere be made Choice of 
to the said Office of a Messenger; and that our present 
Messengers do endeavour to prevail with Bro. Geere to 
accept of the said Office, and, if he accept it, to ordain him 
as soon as conveniently they can. 

A cordial & fatherly Letter being received from Bro. 
Miller & read, it was order'd by this Assembly that Bro. 
Randall give the Thanks of this Assembly to Bro. MiUer 
for the same. 

That the Thanks of this Assembly be given to Bro. 
Beck for his Agreeable Sermon which was done accordingly 
by Bro. Pyall. 

That this Assembly be adjoum'd to the Wednesday in 
the Whitsun Week, 1746, at Bro. Fry's Meeting House in 
Horslydown Southwark, and that Bro. Bush do preach a 
Sermon at the Opening of the Assembly and in Default Bro. 
Pyall.

Conclusions and Agreements at this Assembly, viz. 
[Officers.] Bro. Chilton Chairman 

^"■^-ISall } Moderators 

1- Pursuant to the Asrreement last Year concerning Bro. 

[Geere postpones ^,.., ■, r rf i 

acceptance.] Geere s being chosen to the Messengers office, the present 
Messengers have treated with him, & find that he cannot 
accept it at present; the particular Reasons were not related 
to the Assembly, but the Assembly did acquiesce in the 
Messengers Report, that they were Reasons satisfactory to 

CPage76.] them, confiding in his generous Offer to assist *Bro. Copper, 
upon Occasion, in the mean Time, & hoping & expecting 
that las soon as those Reasons or Obstacles shall be removed, 
Bro. Geere will consent to be ordain'd to the Messengers 
Office. 

2- A Motion being made of Bro. Randall's Election to the 

[Messenger -^ 

elected.] Mcsscngers Office, the Assembly did agree, & have made 

Choice of him to the same Office, & have appointed Bro. Fry, 
Morris, Neal, Treacher, Brittain & Gladman to treat with & 
desire the Church at Mill Yard to consent to it, & also to 
desire Bro. Randall to accept of the same and that they do 
take the best Measures they can to remove all Objections 
that may arise upon that Affair, & to report the Conclusion 
to the next Assembly.^ 

3' Agreed that the Thanks of this Assembly be given to 

Bro. Bush for his agreeable Sermon at the Opening of the 
Assembly, which was done accordingly by Bro. Chilton. 

*■ Agreed that this Assembly be adjourned to the Wednes- 

day in the Whitsun Week 1747 at Bro. Fry's Meeting House, 
Horslydown, Fair Street, and that Bro. Pyall do preach a 
Sermon at the Opening of the Assembly and in Default 
Bro. Copper. 

^- Agreed that the Assembly Book be deposited in the 

Hands of Bro. Fry.

well the state of religion in the churches, and in the country at large.

Agreed 

In Relation to the Agreem,ent last Year cxsnceming a 
Catechism for the Instruction of Children, that Work having 
been Neglected, 'tis the^ Request and Agreement of this 
Assembly that Bro. Morris do undertake it and print one 
as soon as possibly he can, that we may have such a Cate- 
chism to disperse next Assembly, if not sooner. 

*In relation to the kequest of the Letter from Bucking- 
ham Association held at Wendover, desiring some Messengers 
to come down to Chesham to ordain Thomas Sexton one of 
the Elders of Barkhamsted to the Messengers Office, it is 
agreed that Bro. Randall and Bro. G«ere do go down, and 
that they do give timely Notice to the Church, when they 
can go to perform the same. 

Reed, and read a Letter from the Lincolnshire Associa- 
tion, to which they desire an Answer, the Assembly agreds 
that Bro. Morris and Bro. Killingworth do perform the Same : 
which Letter was read and approv'd, and agreed that the 
Minutes of the Assembly be sent to them with the said 
Letter. 

Reed, and read a Letter from Headcorn, and agreed 
that Bro. Neal and Bro. Dearling do draw up a Letter to 
answer the same, which being done, their Letter was read 
and approved. 

Reed, and read a Letter from Leicestershire Assotiation, 
which was left to Bro. Fry to answer

Goode; and that this Assembly be adjourn'd to the Wed- 
nesday in the Whitsun Week in the Year 175 1, at Mr. Fry's 
Meetinghouse in Horslydown, Southwark. 

Agreed, that the Assembly Book be deposited in the 
Hands of Bro. Fry.

Agreed as follows 

According to the Second Agreement last Year Bro. 
Randall and Bro. Geere went down to Chesham, and ordain'd 
Bro. Saxton to the Messengers Office. 

A Report being made from the Association held at 
Bessels Green the 28th Instant by Bro. Copper and Bro. 
Pyall that Bro. Thomas Harrison was by them chosen to 
serve the Churches in the Office of a Messenger; .which 



' Opposite this name stood, Conningsby, which was cancelled.

choice is highly approved of by this Assembly: Agreed 
that Bro. Fry and Bro. Killingwiorth do draw up a Letter, 
and that the said Letter be presented toi the Church of 
Bessells Green by Bro. Copper and Bro. Benge in order to 
press them to yield him up, and Bro. Harrison to accept 
the said Office. 

The Case of Bro. Amos Harrison's going going down to 3. 

Bessels Green to preach tO' some People who are separated Besseis Green.] 
from the Church upon some Opinions, and to break Bread 
to 'em, in a separate manner, was consider'd, and 'twas 
judg'd by this Assembly, that Brother Harrison's Conduct 
therein has been irregular, from wihich he is desired to 
desist for the future, and that for the Time to come he do 
use his best Endeavours (and our Friends at Bessells Green 
also on their Parts, do the same) to unite that People. 

Agreed that the Assembly be adjourned to the Wed- 
nesday in the Whitsun Week, 1752, at Bro. Fry's Meeting 
House, and that Bro. Geere do preach a Sermon at the 
Opening of the Assembly, and in Default Bro. Saxton.

proceedeth as follow;eth 

*The Names and Proceedings of ye Assembly met by 
Adjurnment on June 5: 1754 

Whereas it was desired by ye Lincolnshire Association 
that some Messenger might be Sent dowji to ordain our 
Bro. Gilbert Boyce into ye Mjessenger office which tho. not 
Perform : d by Sending Down those from those Parts has 
been accomplished by Bro. Johnson who's Place of residence 
was more Convenient^ 

A Letter being reed, from Bro. Sturch of Portsmouth 
relating to ye spread of Pernicious Principles in Religion & 
his apprehensions of its fatal consequences it was agreed 
that Bro. Morris be Desired to write an Answer to it & Send 
to Portsmouth as soon as conveniently he can 

A Letter was reed, from Bro. Johnson relating to ye 
Publication of Some Sermons of his it is agreed to request 
Bro. Randall to write an Answer to him with ye Sentiments 
of this Assembley on that Subject 

A Letter was reed, from Lincolnshire Association to 
which Bro. John Neale was Desired to send ye Sentiments 
of the Assembley 

A Letter was reed, from Ditcherling Relating to Church 
Dissipline and it was agreed, that Bro. Fry be Desired to 
write an answer to inform them of ye Sentiments of this 
Assembly 

*Order'd that this Assembly be adjoiar'd to the Wed- 
nesday in Whitesun Week in 1755. & that Bro. Tho. Harrison 
be Desired to Preach a Sermon at ye Opening of ye 
Assembley at that time and on Default Bro. Gilbert Boyce 

Order'd that this Book be kept in ye Hands of Bro.

On reading Bror Johnson's Letter & the Minutes of last [Johnson's ms.

that Brothers Randall Morris & Fry wiith such others as they

his Manuscript & send him their Opinion and Resolution 
thereupon before next Assembly 

On hearing a Case from Lincoln Association relating ^^^^ Mlrriage 
to the Marriage Act & for trying to Obtain such Liberty of ^**J 
Marrying in tl^eir own Churches as is allowed to the Quakers 
Agreed That it be referr'd to Brors. Matthw. Randall & 
Jno. Neal to Enquire & Advise them about this Matter as 
soon as they can^ 

On reading a Letter from Leicester Association & rLeicesti and 
another from; Yaijmouth Complaining of the Want of 3'^^™°^^'!*^' 
Proper Assistant in the Ministry of the Gospel at each place 
Agreed That our Brethren in the Country do look out for 
Proper Persons to Supply these Churches & if any are found 
to send Immediate Advice thereof to Brothers Randall & 
Fry.fi 

^ Until 1754 the state of the law was uncertain. At Common Law the 
mere consent of the parties, expressed in writing or by speech or perhaps even 
by act, was good for many purposes. That common law still holds in some 
states of America, as foreigners occasionally discover to their unbounded 
amazement. And the similarity of the Scots law popularized Gretna Green. 
But Lord Hardwicke's Act obliged a certain notice, and the celebration in the 
buildings of the Established Church. Exceptions were made for Jews and for 
Quakers, and the Lincolnshire Association naturally thought the exception 
might be extended. It was not conceded for many years. 

* In this year some ardent evangelists in Leicestershire became convinced 
of Baptist principles. They needed to be baptized, the Assembly churches 
needed ministers ; but the lock and the key never fitted. Leicester was indeed 
supplied next year, and Wood gives the names J. Johnson and Samuel 
Durance. The latter however soon went to Boston, and this appeal was 
renewed in 1758 and 1761. Except for an occasional visit from Richard Green,

On Reading 

The Minutes of the Last Assembly the first of which 
relating to the Request of the Church at South Carolina for 
a Learned and Faithful Minister Bror Grantham Killingworth 
Inform'd this Assembly That he had Apply'd to Brother 
Danil. Wheeler 

*Who had Accepted the Invitation and he had Rec'd In- 
formation that our said Bror Danl. Wheeler was safely Arrived 
amongst our Brethren there 

It is Resolved by this Assembly that nothing relating 
to the Second Article agreed to last year be at any time 
Introduced or entered Upon by us till after the Business which 
Properly belongs to the Churches of our Lord Jesus Christ 
are Over, and that Brother Fry do Write to our Brethren at 
Bessells Green On that Account.

the Office of Messenger. The Following is; a Testimonial 
Concerning Our Brothers Frys having been Ordained to the 
Said Office 

Whereas The General Assembly holden at Horsely 
Down On Wednesday the ist of June 1757 did imani- 
mously Agree to Chuse and Call to the Messengers 
Office Our Brother Samuel Fry & he hath accepted his 
Choice & Call 

This is to Testify to all whom it may Concern that 
our Said Bror Samuel Fry by Fasting Prayer and Laying 
on of Hands was Ordain'd a Messenger in the Churches 
on the nth day of September 1757 at Canterbury

*After Prayer and Reading the first Paragraph of the [Page 115.] 
Proceedings of this Assembly the Last Year a Lettejr [south 
"was read from Stone and Charles Town in South Carolina 
knd it was Jointly agreed to send a Letter in answer to it 
and Bro. Killingworth was unanimously requested to draw 
up an Answer to it to be likewise Signed by Bro Fry Broor 
Treacher Bror Brittain & Bro. Dobson in behalf of the 
Assembly &c. 

Upon Reading the Fourth Article of the Want of a [Smaiiburgh,] 
Minister at Smalborough this Assembly was informed of 
John Ellis of Turners Hill who was deem'd Capable, dis- 
pos'd and Could be Spar'd from thence Provided the People 
at Smalborough could mak^ a proper Provision for his En- 
couragement of which Brother Killingworth was desir'd 
further to Inform himself 

A Letter from the Leicester Association was read [Eari shuton 

building.] 

Acknowledging their Unity with this Society and assuring 
us that the Land on which a Baptist Meeting was to be built

Harrison to be Chairman in this Assembly and of Bror Sexton

last Assembly was Read and it appear 'd to the Satisfaction 
of this Society that the Several Articles had been properly 
attended to by Persons Deputed. 

A Letter was read by Bror Saml. Edwards desiring to 
have the further opinion of this Asse;cnbly relating to his 
Catechism and of their being made proper use & it was Con- 
cluded to Confirm to him in a Second Letter the Approbation 
of such of the Society whc> had read it and assure him of 
their hearty Esteem of him for his good Intention and wishing 
him great Success to his present Scheme of Opening a Lecturs 
for wch. purpose Bro Fry & such other Friends as he might 
Chuse were desir'd to Write to him. 

With Regard to the Second Article Viz. Inviting Bro. 
Dobell & Bro. Evershed to accept the Ofifice of Messenger 
The Sa^e appears to be Complied with in Conformity to the 
SoUicitation of Friends Provided they had the Unanimous 
Consent of the Respective Societies to wihich they at present 
Stand more Immediately Related and the Joint Voice of the 
Assefnbly. which was Immediately Confirm'd.

being read setting forth the Necessity of rebuilding the same 
and Requesting that it be Recommended by this Assembly

tributions as the Importance of the Case Require and their

^ Except for two more letters, this Association has given its last sign of 
life. In 1762 the self-baptized Methodists of Leicestershire organized into five 
churches linked into one fellowship. In 1770 they united with three from the 
Lincoln Association and some from London, Kent and Essex to form " An

The Proceedings of the Last Assembly were Read after 
Prayer 

Enquiry being made how the first Article thereof was 
Performed to which Brother Fry Answered that a Letter 
had been sent to him accordingly and he also Inform'd the 
Assembly that he had reced. anr. Letter from Brother Saml. 
Edwards which was Produced and read accordingly & the 
Thanks of this Assembly desired to be sent him for his Love 
& Zeal 

Brother Killingworth read the Answer he at the Request 
of Last Years Assembly sent to Bror Wheeler in South 
Carolina & ^it was^ approved. 

Brother Fry Inform'd this Assembly that he had reced. 
a Letter from Brother Thomas Harrison (wjho by Infirmity 
of Body is prevented from attending here) which was read, 

Assembly of Free-Grace General Baptists " better known as the New Con- 
nexion. And in 1771 the Leicester and Lincoln churches formed a Northern 
Association within that Assembly. As the Southern collapsed almost at once, 
the new Assembly became identical with the Northern or only Association ; 
the Leicester Methodists were the backbone of this new society, and their 
Association held annual meetings from 1772 onwards. It must not be con- 
founded with the old Leicester Association here mentioned, though it will 
be heard of presently at this Assembly. The Leicester church lost its Elder, 
T. Davye, a scrivener, about 1750, and it is not clear that another was ever 
ordained. Services were no longer held regularly, and the cause would have 
collapsed but for a small endowment. It was rescued by the New Connexion, 
which it joined in 1781. Knipton also in 1801 passed over; in 1808 the old 
Earl Shilton church abandoned the Assembly and merged into the Hinckley 
New Connexion church ; a year or two later a member of Hinckley settling at 
Fleckney founded a new cause which absorbed the old cause at Smeeton after 
a few years. But these four cases, the only links in the county, bring out 
clearly that there was no organic continuity of Association ; the old just ceased 
to meet ; a new one grew so vigorous that it began instituting sub-divisional 
conferences on Methodist lines, Lincolnshire in 1791, Nottinghamshire in 
1803, &c., &c. 

^ Interlined.

' This was the Glass-House Yard church. The same building was often 
used in those times by quite distinct churches, meeting on different days — ^as 
often now in Australia, or at different hours of the same day — as Catholics 
and Protestants in Elsass. Within the next twenty years the expiration of 
leases caused much' shifting and sharing among the London churches, as is 
traced in the Introduction.
