' Dan Taylor was a young man of 26, who in 1762 had broken with the 
Methodists of Halifax and had fitted up a house for worship in Wadsworth. a 
few miles to the west of that town. Becoming convinced that believers should 
be baptized, he applied to a Baptist minister who lived close by, probably 
Joshua Wood of Halifax, or John Thomas of Heptonstall. But they were

staunch Calvinists, and could not enter into such an official relation to an5r 
one who followed Wesley rather than Whitfield in the doctrine of universal 
atonement. There were other Baptist ministers near by, Richard Smith at 
Wainsgate, William Crabtree at Bradford, James Hartley at Haworth^ 
Thomas Ashworth at Gildersome, John Oulton at Rawden, Alverey Jackson- 
at Barnoldswick, Henry Clayton of Salendine Nook, Joseph Piccop of Bacup,. 
Charles Bamford of Oakenshaw, James Winterbottom of Manchester. But 
no single one would baptize a non-Calvinist,, and all they would do was to tell 
him that at Boston in Lincolnshire was a Baptist church where William 
Thompson, just removing from Hull, held doctrines nearer his own. He was^ 
baptized at Gamston, and joined the Lincolnshire Association. During 1763, 
Thompson and Boyce went and ordained him pastor over a newly formed, 
church at Wadsworth, and preparations were made to build at Birchcliff.. 
The Lincolnshire Association backed the case, and now the whole Assembl5r 
agreed to further it. Next year the young man came in person to collect.

' The first time that the existence of a Particular Baptist is recognized. 
Samuel Stennett had succeeded his father Dr. Joseph Stennett at Little Wild 
Street, and had himself been lately created D.D. by Aberdeen. He was a 
Baptist dispenser of the Regium Donum, an annual grant of about :^iooo, 
administered at this time in doles of not more than £5 at the discretion of 
seven ministers and nine laymen, among the poor ministers of the Presby- 
terians, Independents and Baptists. Since 1760 the money did not come 
direct from the hereditary revenues of the crown, but out of a new Civil List

' Since 175s Daniel Noble had been pastor of the Sabbatarian church 
owning the premises in Mill Yard. As the Goodmans Fields church had 
hired them for awhile, he had had good opportunities of understanding the 
Assembly. Now he became Elder also of the Glass-house yard church, and 
this may have facilitated its transfer back to the north of the river, into the 
Paul's Alley premises in the Barbican.

' College education was now becoming an acute question. The Terrill 
endowment at Bristol was soon to be overhauled, so that Baptists in England 
could have a real Divinity Seminary. But the colonists were in advance, both 
as to time and as to scope of plans ; and from this beginning arose Brown 
University.

* The history of Bessels Green is not monotonous. There had been one 
disruption already adverted to in the minutes; in 1747-8 several had been 
dismissed to form a new church. Now in 1766 a young stocking-weaver named 
John Stanger came as assistant to Samuel Benge, opening a school to support 
himself. Stanger belonged to a Northants family known well at Harringfworth, 
Morcott, Slapton and Holcott, all strongly evangelical. His appearance-

crystallized the two parties, and in 1767 Messeng-ers Evershed and Dobel were 
invoked to allay the strife. The attempt was vain ; on the contrary, all Kent 
and Sussex were obliged to face the issue, while the Assembly now advised 
his retirement. He was however backed by Brittain of Church lane, another 
Midland man, as also by Summers of the Park. They came and formed 
Stanger's friends into a new church during September, while preparations 
were made for a new meeting-house and manse. The matter had now 
assumed serious dimensions, and next year led to a similar disruption in the 
whole Assembly, the plans for which were matured at Lincoln in Michaelmas. 
* All but three are in one column. 

' Interlined. 

' Whether these were all really present is not quite clear. The historians 
of the New Connexion say that Thompson of Boston, Poole of Fleet, Summers 
of the Park, Stanger of Bessels Green, Taylor of Wadsworth, and the men 
marked as present here, met this same day at Church Lane and sent a deputa- 
tion to announce their secession. Apparently the deputation contained three 
Elders, Brittain of Church Lane, Wilkins of Halstead, and Knott of Eythom, 
with other representatives. None of the men here marked signed these 
minutes.

The Case deliver'd by Mr. John Brittain &' signed tsecMsion of 
by others,* was read & We are very sorry that any of our churches.] 
Bretheren in Union with us in this Assembly establishd on

should make any particular Sentements & Interpretations of 
Scripture a Plea for their Seperation from us^ — We heartily

^ The words, others signd, followed and were cancelled. 

* John Brittain is reg'arded as head of the seceders. He was now sixty 
years old, had been known at the Assembly for fifteen years, and was pre- 
siding over a church of about 200 members, one of the oldest in the Assembly 
Summers had been a member at his church till two years ago, and naturally 
followed his lead. The Lincolnshire men could be left to Boyce, who really 
exerted himself to heal the breach. Young Stanger and young Taylor had 
not impressed themselves on the Assembly. 

* This was the old plea of 1696 and 1707. Two secessions had been 
remedied, and it seemed likely that a third might be. The new factor of a 
vigorous evangelistic group in Leicestershire, and a vigorous leader in York- 
shire, soon to come to London, belied the teachings of the past. It may be 
remembered, as Methodist influence was responsible for this rupture, that the 
Methodist Confererice of this year witnessed a rupture between the Calvinists 
and the Arminians, never healed. 

^ This liberty was acted upon. Church Lane and Park never rescinded 
their secession, though they were apathetic in their new relations. Church 
Lane members evidently attended as spectators for some years, till a shade of 
irritation appears in the minutes. Park was soon wrecked by Summers 
seceding to the Calvinists. White's Alley was slumberous, holding only one 
meeting a week for worship, on Sunday afternoon, and recording few church 
meetings after 1745, none after 1761 till its pastor died in 1797. The Assembly 
therefore had only two effective churches left in London. 

' The words, and on failure, follow and are cancelled.

" As an offset to the recent secession, White's Alley had come again into 
fellowship after long absence. In 1736 there had been a momentary appear- 
ance, as also in 1705, but the estrangement dated from 1696. On the death of 
MuUiner in 1739, John Ashworth had been called from Nantwich; on his 
death in 1742, Charles Bulkley had succeeded, and he was destined after 29 
years already to survive till 1797. The reunion was facilitated by the fact that 
" White's Alley " church had hired this very Barbican meeting-house for the 
part of Sunday since 1745. Yet it may well be observed that no one except 
Bulkley attended, and that his attendance caused no comment or minute — 
indeed even this very entry was squeezed in afterwards. 

' This church was meeting on alternate Sunday afternoons in Pinners' 
Hall off Old Broad Street. This building was leased from the Pinners' 
Company by an open-membership Calvinistic church, which had had at least 
two Baptists as pastors, George Fownes and James Foster, while in its 
membership were such famous Particular Baptists as the HoUis family, 
benefactors of Harvard in Massachusetts. But this congregation had sublet 
the building on Saturday to the Calvinistic Sabbatarian Baptists under 
Bampfield. And it had sublet the building on Sunday afternoons to a series 
of congregations. The latest of these, which took possession about 1740, was 
shepherded by John Weatherley from Taunton, a disciple of Joseph Jeffries 
senior : it was descended from the great Particular Baptist church of Petty 
France, and despite the absorption of a secession from White's Alley, was 
Calvinistic in 1717. But Dr. Kinch from Paul's Alley seems to have weakened 
this attitude, and Weatherley carried the movement into indiflferentism. On 
his death a General Baptist from Deptford succeeded as pastor, and two more 
General Baptists soon appeared, Isaac Kimber from Nantwich and Joseph 
Jeffries junior from Taunton. The latter was now the pastor. The prosperity 
of the church was such that it now contented itself with a fortnightly meeting, 
and sub-sub-let the building on alternate Sunday afternoons to the homeless 
Horsleydown church.

* Corrected from, Tho. 

' This is a new departure. Hitherto only churches or associations were 
admitted members ; now a personal member is admitted. The practice is 
deliberately recog-nized and regulated at the present day. The significance of 
this act at this time is considerable. In the desire to strengthen the Assembly, 
the old tenets were no longer upheld : the Confession of 1660 is not mentioned, 
and even the Six Principles are not alluded to now. Jeffries and his father 
were popularly termed Socinians. But Jeffries had a wider horizon than most 
of the members, having been a member of the General Body of Dissenting 
Ministers since 1756, and so concerned in politics as they affected dissenters. 
This is reflected in the next minutes.

for Tutors & School Masters & to procure an Exemption from

' This application had arisen from a Unitarian movement within the 
Church of England to abolish subscription to the XXXIX. articles. In the 
course of its defeat, hints were thrown out that dissenters might be relieved 
from the obligation to subscribe. A bill was accordingly drafted, but rejected 
by the Lords. In the agitation that followed, three Baptist champions were 
Dr. Samuel Stennett of Wild Street, Robert Robinson of Cambridge, and 
Joshua Toulmin of Taunton. The bill was passed into law in 1779.

* This is remarkable in two respects : the church had been in fellowship 
before, had not formally withdrawn, but was formally admitted: a year's 
notice was not required. The cause probably dates from 1673, though no 
Baptist seems to have been licensed then. In 1693 there were nearly 100 
members, and by 1712 they had acquired the "Jews Well" public-house, 
which they licensed for worship. Representatives at the Assembly often 
appeared between 1697 and 1741, the last being apparently the same Ham 
Stacey who now attends. He does not seem to have been ordained till 1789, 
he died that year, and the place was closed. It was presently reopened under 
other auspices, and did not renew its union with the Assembly.

' There had been a tendency to accept the title of Messenger without 
really looking: after the churches. Dobel and Boyce had nobly revived the 
older conception, and though they were not founding many new churches, 
were holding together some, and trying to reclaim others. One result is that 
two London brethren leave the way open for others to take up this work. 

' The Lincolnshire Association now included Coningsby, Fleet, Gosbertoni 
Killingholm, Knipton, Maltby, Monksthorp, Spalding and Tetney. This year 
the old Leicestershire Association met for the Icist time, including Earl Shilton, 
Leicester, Mountsorrel, Smeeton and Wymeswould. The Assembly of the 
New Connexion had in 1771 divided into two Associations, of which the 
southern had speedily died. But its northern Association, of .seven churches, 
met this year at Boston, the solitary church which had permanently passed 
over from the Assembly to the New Connexion. It was obvious that the 
Lincolnshire Association and Boyce should have the moral support of the 
Assembly.

uncertain in its doctrines as to make overtures to the Kent and Sussex 
Particular Baptist Association ; the two London churches were isolated, and. 
the only apparent danger was in the Midlands. The New Connexion had 
drawn up six articles, some of which were expressly meant to negative views 
current in the Assembly. These were evidently tendered now as a basis of 
union, and were at once refused. It is curious that the Leicestershire Conference: 
of the New Connexion had in 1775 disapproved of Articles being used as a test 
for membership ; apparently Taylor was responsible for such a use. Mean- 
while the Maltby church had been divided by the new movement, Gamston 
was inclining that way, Ashford in Derbyshire was imploring money help from 
the Leicestershire Conference in numerous letters, and Poole was begging help- 
thence for his new meeting-house at Long Sutton. The long negotiations were 
near culminating, and Thompson of Boston was bringing the Leicestershire- 
preachers to Gosberton for a conference on May 27 and 28 with a deputation 
from the Lincolnshire Association. But the representatives of the New 
Connexion declined to admit that Messengers and Laying on of hands were- 
of divine obligation, and refused to join the Assembly. 

' The words, general Baptist, follow and are cancelled. 

" The London weakness is evident from this decision. Three churches, 
occupied two buildings, shared by other churches ; and all three were about to- 
be evicted, so that they contemplate borrowing from their Sabbatarian friends; 
at Mill Yard, who were ministered to by Noble of Glass-house Yard.

t This may be contemporary. It may be a note showing the Messengers 
which churches needed visitation, as they sent neither representatives nor 
subscriptions. 

^ Berkhamsted was now served by Elders Caleb Cock and William 
Thrussell, assisted by Joseph Pyall from Headcorn. Apparently there was 
some dissatisfaction with their ministry, or they were not numerous enough for 
all the preaching places, for the church applied this year to the Leicestershire 
Conference of the New Connexion for supplies. But nothing further came of 
this till Thrussell's removal in 1796, except that in 1785 he asked for money 
help from the same Conference. Whether or when Cock became Elder is not 
clear ; the entries in this book are curious.

[Signatures.] 



A Report having been made by Dr. Jeffries of the Pro- 
ceedings of the Committee for procuring farther Relief for 
dissenting Ministers & Schoolmasters; &, that thfe Act, for 
that Purpose had now! pass'd both Houses of Parliament, & 
obtain'd the royal Assent. 

Resolved, that this Assembly, rejoicing at so great ex- 
tension of religious Liberty, & highly approving the Measures 
taken by the said Committee; desire that *their Thanks 
be presented to the said Committee, for the Earnestness & 
Zeal .with which they have persevered in their Application 
to Parliament; 

Resolved that Dr. Jeffries be desired to present the 
thanks of this Body to the said Committee. 

Whereas it .wias inserted, in the Minutes of the last 
Assembly, that the Congregations, meeting in this Place, 
had agreed to renetw'. the Lease of it; Mr. Lowdell no!w) 
reported, that the Land Lord of the said Place having 
afterwards^ refused to comply with the Terms^ propos'd and 
agreed on, A piece of Freehold Ground has therefore since 
been purchased, in Worship Street, near Bishops-gate Street ; 
and its propos'd to build a new Meeting house there as soon 
as possible : & the said Congregations, likewise with Pleasure, 
acknowledge the farther assistance received from their Friends 
in the Country, during the Course of last Year. 

Agreed that this Assembly do meet on Wednesday in 
Whitsunweek at the Meeting House at Barbican in the Year 
1780. Service to begin at half past Ten and that Brother 
Seaton do preach & on his Default^ Brother Bolton, & on his 
Default^ Bror. Finch. 

Agreed, that this Assembly Book be deposited in the 
Care of J. Brown.

* This building was erected for the joint occupancy of four churches ; the- 
three Assembly churches which kept the historic names of White's Alley, 
Horslydown, Glass-house Yard, and Jeffries' church from Artillery Lane 
which had been with Brown's church at Pinner's Hall and Bury Street- 
This last church lost its pastor in two years, and seems to have quietly died or- 
amalgamated at some date after 1792. The building- then became the property 
of the three churches, and so remained even after two of them moved away 
and left the old Glass-house Yard church in sole occupation. The country- 
churches which had often been helped in their building cases were now rallying- 
well to the help of the London churches. Subscriptions amounted to ;^i,700-- 
within five years, and the balance of ;£30O was met by a loan from Ramsgate, 
where Thomas and Ann Piety had in 1768 given money for the support of the; 
local ministry.

LEICESTERSHIRE AND NORTHAMPTONSHIRE. 

The churches in these counties had been slowly fading away, and seeing 
that no Messenger helped them they were beginning to look to the New 
Connexion. This year the Leicester church, which had depended on Richard 
Green, Elder of Earl Shilton, for occasional visits, applied to the Leicestershire 
Conference at Melbourne for preaching in the Friar Lane meeting-house. 
Supplies were sent, and John Deacon, who had studied under Dan Taylor, 
proved so acceptable that the church reorganized next year and called him as 
regular preacher. The result was that in 1783 they formally joined the New 
Connexion : for several years Deacon came from the " Leicester Association " 
as representative with Dan Taylor, but there are signs from 1793 of " inexplic- 
able difficulties " at Friar Lane, as if Deacon were too old-fashioned for some ; 
and a secession took place with the approval of the Leicestershire Conference. 
Whatever the precise nature of the trouble, it never led to Friar Lane rejoining 
the Assembly, or even to its quitting the New Connexion. 

The case of North Moulton is equally pathetic. Thomas Stanger, who 
had attended Assembly in 1761 and 1765, died in 1768. For thirteen years 
the widow had kept the people tog'ether, but now the cause seemed desperate. 
William Stanger at Morcott was too far away, so one of the family attended 



' Another ex-Calvinistic church opening communications. 
■• Added. Daniel Dobel died this year, and his son Benjamin was now 
recommended as Messenger in his stead. 

* All names but Bulkley's form one column on page 191. 



Worship Street] ASSEMBLY 173 

the Leicestershire Conference at Hinckley this year, and desired that the 
church might be received into fellowship ; the case was deferred, and the doors 
had absolutely to be closed after a century. Help came in a few years from 
quite another quarter, when a cobbler at Hackleton, only 24 years old, was 
called to the ministry by the Particular Baptist church at Olney— itself an 
evolution from a General Baptist cause ? — and found such opportunities here 
that in 1786 he was ordained by Ryland of Northampton, Fuller of Kettering-, 
and his own pastor, Sutcliff of Olney. If these were of another school, yet a 
link with the past was preserved by John Stanger, son of the late pastor, 
coming from Bessels Green to assist. And though Ryland was so intensely 
Calvinistic that he openly rebuked the young man for proposing to teach all 
nations, elect or non-elect. Fuller was of a more genial theology. Fostered by 
his teaching young William Carey wrote at Moulton in that same year, " An 
Enquiry into the Obligations of Christians to use Means for the Conversion of 
the Heathens, &c." Surely the 'old traditions of the church created an 
atmosphere around him, when the ancient Confession declared openly that 
Christ had commanded the glad tidings of remission of sins to be preached to 
every creature that they might be saved, and inferred that all men at one time 
or another were put into suchja capacity, as that (through the grace of God) 
they might be eternally saved. Nevertheless it must be sorrowfully acknow- 
ledged that the midland churches which had so sturdily upheld these principles, 
perhaps from the days of John Smith and the Coventry church of 1624, could 
find no inducement to abide in the ancient fellowship, but were seeking new 
associates. When William Stanger died at Morcott in 1790, the fine old 
family had none to succeed him, and the ancient church made overtures to the 
Leicestershire Conference.

The Minutes of the Proceedings of the last Assembly 
were read & confirmed. 

Reported by the Messengers Brothers Boorman & 
Evershed that they have ordained Brothers Dobel, Sampson 
Kingsford & *Noble, as appointed at the last Assembly since [Page m] 
which Time we have received the melancholy Account of 
the Death of our Brother Noble. 

We still recommend to all the Churches, that, if any 
of them have a Member or Members, Hearer or Hearers, 
residing near any of our Sister Churches, that the Pastor 
Minister or Deacon do write to the Pastor, Minister or 
Deacon of such Church near whom they reside desiring them 
to take christian Notice of such Persons. 

As some of our Churches have neglected to send recom- 
mendatory Letters with their Representatives, such Churches 
are requested for the future to send such^ Letters with them. 

On a proposal of union from the Leicester Association [Overtures from 
(by Brothr. Dan. Taylor) with this Assembly under par- conneSm.] 
ticular Restrictions, it was agreed to refer the Case to our 

' Not the old Leicestershire Association, which was last heard from in 
1766, and which met as lately as 1776, according to Wood, page 149. This is 
the " Northern Association " of the New Connexion, as it styles itself in the 
letter appended; it had recently held its annual meeting at Kirton-in-Lindseyi 
an old General Baptist church which had quitted its immemorial associates 
for the New Connexion in 1779. The Northern Association had already 
gleaned a secession church at Maltby, Grantham's old church at Yarmouth, 
and the outlying cause at Ashford in Derbyshire : this year it was strengthened 
by Leicester and Gosberton, while troubles at Wisbech were on the verge of 
giving them the church there. 

^ Daniel Taylor was no longer a raw youth who could be overlooked. 
Aged 36, he was the soul of the New Connexion. For one year Thompson of 
Boston had been put in the chair at its Association meeting, but on every 
other occasion, even in Thompson's own meeting-house, Taylor presided.

* This addendum is interlined. The letter is appended in this edition at 
the close of this year's minutes. 

' Originally, spiritual. 

° Sampson Kingsford was evidently profiting by the catechetical plan of 
the Methodists, requiring answers to definite questions, instead of letting 
churches write what news they liked. Next year he presided at Ramsgate 
over a visitation of that church, and asked the following questions, probably 
selected from his plan : — (i) Weather there be any Differances in your Church 
that are thought proper or that you are authorised to propos to this Meeting 
to be settled by us. (2) Is their any member that do not behave in an orderly 
and Reputable manner among you and are such Noticed and Reprovd 

(3) Wether a Regard be paid to the improvement of youth and Wether the 
Promising Gifts for the Ministry among them be Tryd and Encoraged 

(4) Wether your Chuch be on the increase or decline and what is your present 
number. (5) Is the word of God Statedly and Constantly Administered 
amongst you and do your Members steadely Attend the word Thus preachd 
(6) Are your poor and Needy Members attended to with Brotherly Care and 
tenderness. Secular Buisness. Wether there be any Masters or Missteress 
want Servants, or Journemen and apprentices want places and wether there 
be any opening for Buisness In our Connection.

*The following is the Copy of Letter sent to the General 
Assembly Meeting in Worship Street London at Whitsontide 
in the Year 1784 

Dear Friends and Brethren 

We who Compose the Northern Association^ take 
the freedom to Inform you that we have received some 
Copies of Mr. Kingsford's plan,^ and have taken some 
pains in considering the Design and Contents of it. Upon 
Mature reflection, we apprehend that it is Calculated, if 
properly put in Execution, with such Improvments as 
you will be able to add, to promote the spiritual Welfare 
of the Churches in General, and the temporal advantage 
of many Individuals, in several parts of the Kingdom.^" 
Viewing it in this Light, we cant but wish Success to 
every prudent Effort towards the Execution of it; and 
so far as may be Judged right, both by You and us, 
when such matters are settled as will be necessary for 
the Union supposed in the plan, wie wish to Join in these 
Efforts.'' 

But in order, bretheren, that every one may recieve 
full satisfaction, that w:e all Act, with Integrity, — that 
nothing may be disguised, which is necessary to be 
known, and that there may be a real and substantial 
harmony among us all, wle think it may not be improper, 
with Christian freedom to mention a few things, which 
You have a right to know; and the mention of which 
may prevent future Altercation and unhappiness. 

*We apprehend the Plan under consideration does 
not require a perfect Conformity to one another in every 
religious sentiment, though we can assure you, that we 
take a great pleasure in agreeing with You, and with all 
other Professions of Religion, so far as we can. 

Nor do w;e think the plan necessitates all who 
approve of it, and unite in the Execution of it, to be 
exactly agreed in the practice of some forms of religion, 



' The Northern (and really only) Association of the " New Connexion of 
General Baptists formed in 1770." 

' Copies had been sent to the Leicestershire Conference of the New 
Connexion, and on April 14 from the former Assembly church at Friar Lane, 
that Conference referred the consideration to the Association. 

"" The Northern Association had sprung from two Methodist roots, and 
was familiar with the Methodist catechetical plan. 

" The plan was a distinct overture for union.

Now we find ourselves, in conscience, oblig'd to except 
against what we call a close^^ union with all Ministers 
and Churches of this Class, particularly in those two 
instances. We cant think it right to change Pulpits 
with such Ministers as these, or to Encourage them to 
Preach to our Congregations, as we are persuaded that 
their Ministrations must have a pernicious Influence on 
the minds of the people. Nor can we think it safe to 
recieve Members^^ of such Churches intO' our Fellowship, 
in case of removal from lone Country, or part of the 
Kingdom to another, upon the recomendation of the 
Churches to which they belong, 'till we have examined 
the conversion and conversation of those members, who 
may on that account offer themselves to our fellowship.

'^ Because the churches gleaned from the Assembly held to the old lines, 
and the Methodist churches cared nothing about them. It was on this rock 
that the negotiations split. Next year the reply to this letter, given in at 
Boston to the Northern Association, stipulated for Laying on of hands, and 
Abstention from eating blood. See Wood, page 188. Some of the Leicester- 
shire ministers were circulating Boyce's tract on Singing, and were rebuked by 
their brethren next year, as it had been decided that plain four-part tunes, 
unaccompanied, were desirable, and as collections of hymns were already 
being contemplated.

& finally, We find it necessary to Inform You, 
Biretheren, that with all these restrictions, there are 
several Individuals, and some Churches who are not yet 
come to a Conclusion in favour of the Union supposed in 
the plan; and a few; seem to be warm and possitive 
against it. But the time for consideration has been 
short, and what will be the Issue of our present con- 
sultations respecting it, w:e cannot pretend to know. 
And we who compose this Association are happy and 
harmonious, and would earnestly wish to proceed with 
Caution, lest anything should open a way to alienate 
our affections from each other and therefore We 
apprehend it to be most eligible, to desire a little more 
time to consider of the matter, before any of us commence 
a formal Union with the General Assembly. But *But 
we supposed it proper, to Lay these things fairly before 
You, as an evidence of our sincere affection for all 
who Love our dear Savior, and our cordial wishes for 
the prosperity of his Interests, and the Success of every 
project that may contribute thereto. 

We Conclude with assuring You of our ardent desire 
for your prosperity. And we pray that you may be under 
divine direction^ in all the Business of your present 
Assembly.

we still continue to request that if any of them have^ a 
Member or Members, Hearer or Hearers, residing near any 
of our Sister Churches, that the Pastor, Minister or Deacon,

On a proposal of union from Leicestershire Association 
under particular restrictions, &c which was last year referred 
to our several Churches, We all agree that an Union is 
desirable & wie are willing to receive our ^general Baptist^ 
Brethren in Love ^who hold^ the Principles of general Re- 
demption &3 the truths recorded in Heb. 6. i — 2* leaving 
them as to the Articles mentioned in their Letter to their 
own Liberty, and if such an Union on this broad Basis is 
desired, a Basis on which the Assembly for many years hath 
stood, we should think it a joyful Day, and look on it as 
a happy presage of a lasting Union. 

On asking the Questions of the several Churches as 
proposed last year, some answered & some did not. From 
several Churches we had a good account, & we recommend 
to others, who have acknowledged their Deficiencies, by 
their Representatives, to exert themselves in future to support 
discipline, encourage communicants, admonish the earless 
& reprove the unruly.

The Minutes of the Proceedings of the last Assembly 
were read & confirmed. 

A Sermon was preached by Brother Philpot. 

*As by a Letter from the Leicestershire Association, 
they have declared their Willingness to unite with this 
Assembly, as proposed last Year ; We have accepted & agreed 
to such Union, & request for the furtherance of the grand 
Purposes of Religion in general, that they will send their 
Representatives annually amongst us.* 



' This is the second appearance of a seceded church since 1786, and it slips 
back without notice. It had been grouped into the moribund Southern 
Association of the New Connexion. For seven years it is represented here 
again, then called a Particular Baptist minister and vanished over the horizon 
of the Assembly. Deal had slipped back in 1783. 

■* This minute is astonishing. The letter is not recorded in the book, and 
we cannot judge for ourselves. We are certain that the Northern Association 
declined to unite with the Lincolnshire Association, because the latter stipulated 
for certain ritual. And we are certain, by the letter of 1784, that it hesitated 
to unite with the Assembly. But it is clear that the Assembly considered this 
letter of 1786 an acceptance; it is clear that Gilbert Boyce, in 1789, had the 
same impression — " as our brethren of the Leicestershire Association are now 
in connection with the General Assembly in London," Taylor IL 324 — and the 



Worship Street] ASSEMBLY 189 

Finding great Inconvenience and much Loss of Time in ^}^°^f^°''^ 
our asking the religious Questions relative to the State of churches.] 
the separate Churches, as practised in a former Assembly, 
we have agreed & do earnestly recommend, instead of the 
said Questions, that each Church for the future, before they 
send Representatives, do' meet & consider the State of their 
Members & send an Account in Writing by their Reprer 
sentatives, ^as follows^ — What Pastors, Ministers & Deacons 
are amongst them? Whether ,the Word of God is statedly 
& constantly administered & the Members make good their 
Places ? Whether Conferences & Prayer Meetings are kept 
up & Encouragement given to the Exercise of Gifts among the 
Youth? Whether the Church is on the Decline or Increase 
and what are the present Number of Members. 

We have taken under Consideration the Request of the [Normch.] 
Church at Norwich under the pastoral Care of Brother Proud, 
and we think proper, as usual with this Assembly,^ to defer 



minutes of Assembly will show frequent correspondence, frequent representa- 
tion, return of statistics, and a formal withdrawal in 1803. It would appear 
that Adam Taylor was rather ashamed of this coquetting, for he had these 
facts before him, and misrepresents them. And Wood, who had no access to 
these minutes, makes no allusion to the matter. It will be observed that these 
minutes, with this explicit statement, are signed by Dan Taylor, who, in 1785, 
had come to London, and was associated with Brittain in the pastorate of the 
Church-lane church in Whitechapel — the ancient Goodman's Fields congrega- 
tion. The peculiarity of the situation is evident when we remember that this 
church in this year was formally received into the New Connexion, and is not 
represented at the Assembly. When the minutes of " The General Baptist 
Association, held at Boston, Lincolnshire, April 27th and 28th, 1785 " are 
scanned to see how the matter was viewed by the New Connexion, we find the 
minute vague, and not specifying whether the overtures were made by Boyce 
on behalf of the Lincolnshire Association or the General Assembly, though the 
minutes of 1791 imply it was the latter, as Boyce then was negotiating for 
" the union of what are called the Leicestershire and Lincolnshire associations.'' 
The final result was " that nothing more was necessary to effect a reunion than 
their acceeding to the terms mentioned by us at the last association." It is 
distinctly interesting to find that these New Connexion minutes were this year 
printed by J. Brown, at the Printing-Office, Fair Street, Horsleydown, 
Southwark. In 1786 no allusion was made tothe topic. In 1787 it was agreed 
that a friendly letter, with a copy of the minutes, be presented to the General 
Assembly, meeting in Worship Street. But inasmuch as a deputation was 
appointed then to visit the churches of Hertfordshire, Buckinghamshire, 
Bedfordshire, and Huntingdonshire, in which four counties only Wisbech had 
joined the New Connexion, it is not surprising if the friendliness should be 
misinterpreted.

' The Kingsford family was doing now for the cause what the Kingsnoth. 
family had done in the same county a century earlier. 

'In April a great meeting was held at Ramsgate, when Messenger 
Sampson Kingsford ordained George Kingsford as Elder. Ashdown and. 
Philpot of Dover, William Kingsford of Wingham, and Fenn of Deal signed 
as witnesses. But it is more remarkable to find besides Ranger of Eythorn, 
and Atwood of Folkestone, that Purchas was present ; he was a decided. 
Calvinist. The incident illustrates the extraordinary confusion of the time,, 
when Kentish churches wavered between the old Association, the New Con- 
nexion, the Particular Association. The Ramsgate church, however, did not 
waver, and instructed its Elder to go to the Assembly regularly, voting a 
guinea yearly for the expense.

preached as had been appointed a year before, he accepted a post on a com- 
mittee, and he signed the minutes. When we find that John Deacon, pastor- 
of the old Assembly church at Leicester, which had lately joined the New

yearly such Letters, as they are much to the comfort of our

Churches, to support Church discipline, call over the Names

*A Sermon was preached by Brother Deacon from Acts 
26. 17-18. 

On reading the Several Letters from the Churches wis 
have the pleasure to inform you that the Numbers of Hearers 
& Members are increased considerably, & that the Advice 
of this Assembly in pursuing the Means of supporting the 
Power of Religion by Conference &c has been attended with 
success, & we warmly recommend to the Churches, who have 
sent an Account of the State of Religion amongst them, to 
continue such Letters as they are much to the Comfort of 
our Assembly; and wfi again desire those who have omitted 
such Letters, not to neglect them for the future; and we 
further recommend to the Several Churches to keep up 
frequent Meetings to support Church Discipline, call over 
the Names of their Members, and use every Method that may 
tend to revive & mentain the Power of Religion amongst 
them, & particularly that they be earnest^ in private Prayer 
for themselves & their Ministers. 

A Minister is still wanting at Saffron Walden in Essex, 
and the Meeting houses at Winslow & Wickham in Bucks are 
unoccupied: A minister is also wanting at Fleet, in Lincoln- 
shire. ^ 

Being desirous of ascertaining the Number of General 
Baptists in this kingdom we request our Brethren Dan 
Taylor & Sampn. Kingsford to make such Enquiries as they 
are able, and make report of the Same to the next Assembly. 

The Committee appointed last Year by this Assembly, 
report they have waited on the general Committee to carry 
in a Petition to the House for the Abolition of the Slave 
trade, & that Mr. Pitt has declared his Intention tO' bring it 
forward next Sessions. 

As it appears from the Accounts given in by the several 
Churches, that the cause of Religion has gained ground, in 
a very considerable degree. We could wish that our Brethren 
in Connection will appoint a Day for the purpose of thanks- 
giving to God for this inestimable blessing; and that they 
will unite in fervent prayer that this may be continued in 
future. We also earnestly request that they will not forget 
to pray the Lord of the Harvest to send forth more Labourers, 
and give Success to those already raised.

' William Foot, after ministering to Tiverton, now a Particular Baptist 
church, and to Moreton Hampstead, now in touch with the Assembly, had in 
1736 gone to a church at Callow Hill Street in Bristol. This was of a peculiar 
type, its most characteristic feature being a tendency to Socinianism. There 
he ministered till his death in 1782. He had published many works, including 
a Practical Discourse concerning Baptism, in 1739 and 1750, and a Plain 
Account of the Ordinance of Baptism in 1766. The latter is in question ,; the 
reprint has a biography appended.

' Lowdell had some energy ; next year he annotated this book, and he was 
instrumental in reviving White's Alley from its coma.

^ Robert Robinson was pastor of the Calvinistic Baptist Church at St. 
Andrew's Street, Cambridg-e, which he had induced to admit unbaptized 
persons to communion. He was showing decided leanings to Socinianism, and 
next year preached his last sermon to Priestley's Unitarian congregation, 
exciting Priestley's surprise at his ridicule of the doctrine of the Trinity.

' The only systematic attempt yet to train Baptists for the ministry was at 
Bristol, and though the influence of Caleb Evans was peculiar, this was not a. 
General Baptist institution. So far the rigid Calvinistic Baptists and the New- 
Connexion had not taken any steps in this direction.

' Birley was a scion of the one Derbyshire church, but had been associated 
since with Dan Taylor at Birchcliff, and with John Ryland at Northampton — 
facts which make us despair of rigid classification. His very catholicity 
doubtless helped in transforming- the churches of Burton Latimer, North 
Moulton, Spratton, and Stoney Stratford. Another illustration of the mixture 
of parties is seen in that when he was ordained over St. Ives in 1786, Robert 
Robertson and Dan Taylor officiated, while he soon corresponded with 
Andrew Fuller. He was not likely to teach anj^hing explicit; but as this year 
the old church — a continuation of Fenstanton — joined the New Connexion, and 
he declined this invitation, he did not guide the young ministers of the Assembly. 

■* A revival was proceeding also among the Particular Baptists, due 
doubtless to Fuller and Carey. One token of it is that John Rippon, reviving 
the traditions of Stinton, Crosby, and Thompson, began collecting and pub- 
lishing statistics of his own denomination. He presently got the figures of the 
New Connexion, but asked in vain for facts about the Assembly. 

' It is an innovation for only Officers and Messengers to sign ; it became 
a precedent.

' Dobel was entered as Elder here, but the name was cancelled. He was 
Elder, and he was present ; but he ranked as Messenger and as Chairman. 
The Messengers were beginning again to magnify their office, and the process 
went on till at the present day it is explicitly stated that they " are officers of 
the Assembly, appointed by it, and sitting in it independently of their con- 
nection with any church." This, of course, was not the custom hitherto. 
Evershed and Boorman are named here both as Messengers and as Elders.

The Minutes of the Proceedings of last Assembly were

The Report on 2d Article of last Year is — that the Church [Business arising 

_,, ._. ,,. . • 1 1 r 1 TTT- 1 J out of minutes.] 

at Fleet m Lmcomshire is provided for but Winslow and 
Wickham are still vacant. 

*As to the Case of Waldron Church, we recommend again [Page 218.] 
to the attention of Brothers Boorman & Dobel. 

^ This year the Lincolnshire ministers of this Leicestershire Association, 
i.e., the Association of the New Connexion, founded a Lincolnshire Conference. 
The minutes of the second meeting, held at Fleet on Sept. 27, show the 
following Elders present : — From ancient churches came William Thompson 
of Boston, Thomas Mills of March, who had come there from Spratton in 1744, 
Thomas Rusling of Spalding, ordained by Boyce four years before, Samuel 
Wright his assistant, and Thomas Ewen a young preacher at Fleet. From 
churches which already had preachers of the New Connexion, were William 
Burgess of Fleet — deacon of Church Lane or Goodman's Fields in 1785, 
Joseph Binns of Gosberton from Yorkshire, John Smith of Tydd St. Giles from 
Long Sutton, and Joseph Freeston of Wisbech from Leicestershire. The 
singularity of the situation is accentuated by Poole of Long Sutton writing to 
criticise adversely, and by Boyce of Coningsby writing to approve. Fleet 
raised the question whether Laying on of hands were obligatory, and it was 
decided that mutual liberty was desirable. Overtures were made to Coningsby, 
Maltby, Spalding, Fleet and Killingholm, besides Wisbech and Chatteris, and 
it was mooted whether the churches should all be invited to join the " New, or 
Leicestershire Connexion," on the basis of mutual forbearance. The only 
stalwart was William Kelsey of Knipton, the man who as Elder of Ashford had 
repeatedly begged help from the Leicestershire Conference ; he now opposed 
union on the ground that no church could dispense with what was divinely 
ordered. 

' Marsom presently joined White's Alley and gave it a new lease of life — 
the Mitcham church merging. From the White's Alley records comes another 
copy of the minutes for this year, with a list of subscribers to the Fund for 
assisting Ministers and Students of the General Baptist persuasion. The 
places represented were London (Worship Street and Church Lane), Asterby, 
Boston, Burnley, Caldwell, Canterbury, Chatham, Chatteris, Chesham and 
Berkhamsted, Coggeshall, Coningsby, Cranbrook, Ditcherling, Fleet, Gos- 
berton, Guildford, Headcom and Smarden, Horsham, Knipton, Longwood, 
Maidstone, Melbourn, Mitcham, Monksthorp, Moreton-Hampstead, Nutfield, 
Oxted, Queenshead, Ramsgate, Rushall, Saffron Walden, Southwick, Staple- 
hurst, Tetney, Thetford.Wadsworth, and Wisbech. Not a single Leicestershire 
church subscribed.

' This line is added. The bare minutes do not show what was really- 
happening, but the church records show that as soon as Daniel Noble died in 
1783, Charles Bulkley was asked to preach and administer the Lord's Supper, 
until a pastor should be chosen. The arrangement lasted till 1793, when the 
52 members elected John Evans, who with two university degrees entered on a 
busy literary" career. The church was one of the strongest, numbering at this 
time the Lowdells and Treachers in its fellowship. From 1783 to the present 
century it claimed the privilege of catering for the Assembly meetings.

" One welcome result of this direction was the'recovery of the five books 
belonging to White's Alley, which had been disused since 1761. The first of 
these is now best known as the Association Book, and is with the Assembly 
records. The other four are in the Guildhall Library. In each case a note by 
Lowdell is wafered in, detailing the recovery. They were lent to Rippon the 
Particular Baptist historian, who does not seem to have used them. See 
Introduction, II.

' Brother Taylor was busy entertaining the " Liecester '" Association at 
Church Lane; and it was just agreed that it should meet in London every 
eighth year. No representative appeared here. Brittain died this year, and 
Church Lane at once abandoned the Laying on of Hands. How little the New 
Connexion considered itself in fellowship with the Assembly is seen by the 
Circular Letter of 1793 signed by Taylor. Discussing the advantage of 
frequent meetings for conference, it is said, " Is it not a glaring fact, that 
among the old General Baptists, their Cause in many places was nearly 
extinct, through the inattention of their Ministers, and others to these 
measures ? " Both the tone, and the " their " show an attitude of aloofness.

■• This waked up Ramsgate, and the deeds were renewed next year. Six 
years later the old Park Church did actually lose its building and its documents. 

'^ It was a few years since Virginia and South Carolina had been in touch. 
Rippon had lately published statements that "we have a number of General 
Baptists in Kentucke" (I. 117); and that ''nearly one half the inhabitants of 
both Virginia and South Carolina are Baptists " (I. 100). Considering the 
glowing accounts of the Rhode Island College, which had lately conferred 
honorary degrees not only on George Washington and on English Particular 
Baptists, but on William Richards, of Lynn (soon to be met here), and on 
several Taunton men, it was natural to think of sending a deputation. 

^ The " Revd." has come to stay.

^ Mistake for Jos. Joseph Hobbs had been helping Neale at Chatham 
since 1793; in 1795 he took sole charge, being ordained on April 30 by Dan 
Taylor. But the tension was great, and in 1800 he was excluded, whereupon 
he founded a new cause on evangelical lines ; but in 1802 went to Berkhamsted 
to help Sexton, and seven years later took that church out of the Assembly.

preaching near Canterbury. The Leicestershire and Lincolnshire Conferences 
of the New Connexion were communicating with Treacher, " Treasurer of the 
Committee for the Education of young men for the ministry among the 
General Baptists." Lincolnshire was decidedly favourable to the plan, but 
Leicestershire cautiously remitted to the churches, and asked Deacon to print 
his own proposals for education. In 1797 on Dec. 11 a committee met to 
organize for instituting a General Baptist Academy. This rival action was 
one of the matters that strained the singular union of the "Leicester 
Association'' with the Assembly. By 1800 the Academy had 35 agents, 
including the Kingsfords of Deal, Canterbury and Portsea, Hobbsof Chatham, 
Stanger of Fleet, Boyce of Coningsby, and others at such Assembly towns as 
Lyndhurst, Downton, Trowbridge, Fleet, Peterborough, Burgh, Maltby, 
Louth, Killingholm.

^ Bulkley was dead after 54 years as pastor. So, too, was the church, 
-nearly; five women and three men were left, who had not had a church 
meeting since 1761. Lowdell had rescued the books in 1794, and summed up 
the endowments, including- one third (not one fourth) interest in Worship 
"Street, as under a deed of 1781 ; besides a reversion to some property of 
•Glass-House Yard church. The Mitcham church fused with this and started 
■it anew.

" The presence of the New Connexion spectators was apparently beginning

Lincolnshire Association 









' These brethren took a rather disloyal step this year. They united with 
a new comer at the old Park Church to institute a meeting every six months 
on the pattern of the Conferences in Leicester and Lincoln of the New Con- 
nexion. The Park minister soon turned Calvinistic, and helped wreck the 
old cause, but the " London Conference " continued, and gradually made 
further inroads on the Assembly.

Respecting the Church at Deptford it is reported that 9. 

the Place has been opened for Public Worship & it continues opened.] 
to be conducted there twice every Sabbath Day. A favour- 
able prospect therefore may be indulged respecting the 
revival of that place which must be a pleasing circumstance 
to every Individual who wishes well to the General Baptist 
Interest. 5 



' Originally, Agents. 

* The church at Deptford had not been heard of at the Assembly since 
1692 till the enquiry of 1797. It had been represented at the General Associa- 
tion in 1697, William Alcock and William Woodham being Elders, but had 
withdrawn in 1699, when Alcock seems to have passed away. In 1716 a sub- 
scription of £10 was given to rebuild the Baptistery at Horsleydown, from 
" Adam Holden for the Church at Deptford," and when the church nominated 
a trustee, it put forward John Yeomans, who had been at the Association of 
1699. This church must not be confounded with that at Cherry Gardens, 
where Joseph Matthews was pastor, for he is known 1715-1731. Nor must it 
be confounded with an obscure cause served by John Biddle 1723-1749, for he 
was helped from the Particular Baptist Fund. But Ivimey, whose blunders 
and bad reasoning at IV. 231 need not invalidate every statement he makes, 
mentions in Deptford a Mr. Beamont in 1729, "not sound in the faith,'' and a 
Mr. Richard Barron in 1749, who, as we know, went to Paul's Alley five years 
later ; these two may conceivably be successors to Yeomans. The meeting- 
house is mentioned in 1739 by Maitland, under the title " Rotherhithe " ; it 
really stood in Church Lane. The minutes of 1797 imply that the building 
was then disused and likely to be lost. This agrees with the fact that in the 
records of the Kentish Association there was no representative or letter 
between 1768 and 1779, when apparently the very name was dropped. We 
may note in advance that the young student, William Moon, was set to 
resuscitate a cause, that the old Horslydown Church recrossed the river to 
share the building, that on the death of the aged Joseph Brown, the two 
churches united in 1804, under Moon — requesting that a record be made in the 
Kentish book — and that Moon died shortly before the Kentish meetings of 
1824. The building still stands in Church Street, Deptford, where the united 
churches still worship.

Worship Street] ASSEMBLY 241 

Trust it appears that during the Term of Mr. Hunt's Life 
he has the sole power of adding to the present remainilng 
trust so that the number 12 be preserved. On his death the 
society have the power to chose additional Trustees or when 
the Trust are reduced to Three then those three have a 
right to chose a new Trust that all the purposes of the Deed 
may be perpetuated — ^the premises being preserved & the 
profits appropriated to the support of a General Baptist 
Society in that place. We therefore conclude the Assembly 
can* do nothing in the^ business at present unless they agree 
with Mr. Hunt. We therefore appoint our Brethren D. Taylor 
& S. Kingsford to write to Mr. Hunt on that subject & to 
make thier report to the next Assembly.

" Originally, who. 

" William Richards was originally a member of Salem, in Carmarthen- 
shire, who had settled at Lynn in Norfolk. In 1690, Grantham and Marham 
were concerned with a General Baptist church here, but heavy fines had nearly 
broken it up. About 1744, another Baptist church was founded by hyper 
Calvinists, seceding from the Presbyterians. They reorganised in 1760, built 
in 1774, and called Richards in 1777. Joshua Thomas and John Rippon 
returned him as a Particular Baptist from 1789 to 1798, and there is plenty of 
evidence that the church was hyper-Calvinistic till 1818, though it did not 
belong to the Norfolk and Suffolk Association of Particular Baptist churches 
in 1802, Rippon's correspondent admitting that there were some " old churches 
who do not join in Association.'' It is rather more than singular that Richards 
asked help from the General Baptists. The explanation is probably to be 
found in Joshua Thomas' History of the Welsh Baptists, under date 1790 :— 
" There has of late years been some difference in the expressions of certain 
persons upon the confessedly deep and mysterious doctrine of the adorable 
Trinity." 

'* Originally, Churches amongst us, the change being made at once. 

"The New Connexion did, in 1770, adopt this very style, " Free Grace 
General Baptists." The Assembly now challenged this, as implying that the 
remaining churches did not insist upon Free Grace. At the present day the 
Assembly formally acknowledges in its Year-Book, that churches do at 
pleasure add other descriptive phrases.

" Originally, Agreed that this Assembly, the change being made at once. 
" For an account of the church at Parliament Court and Mr. Vidler, see 

■the Transactions of the Baptist Historical Society, Vol. I. number i. The 

•church had been gathered by Elhanan Winchester, in 1787, on the basis of 
Universal Restoration. In 1794 it passed under the care of William Vidler, a 
Particular Baptist from Battle, who had been expelled from the Kent and 
Sussex Association for adopting these views. Under his care most of the 
members became Baptists, as the tenth minute here shows. But by 1791 
Vilder had been discussing Calvinism with Andrew Fuller, evincing a tread 

towards Socinianism. In this year he undertook a propaganda tour in the 
Fens and Lincolnshire, and the influence of Richard Wright at a new Wisbech 
General Baptist Church decided him to avow himself Unitarian. This 

(evolution nearly wrecked his church, but did not hinder him and his church 
being admitted to the Assembly.

recieved by Mr. Jno. Treacher No. 43 Pater nosterow London

it has been the desire of our Friends in the Country to know 
something of the course of Education pursued — it may be 
observed that the principal object of these instructions is in 
the first place to teach them to speak & write the English [Curriculum.] 
Language with propriety — an acquisition of high importance 
to Ministers whose grand employ is to address thier fellow 
creatures in a public capacity. At the same time the Young 
Men are made acquainted with sacred^ & Profane^ History — 
and indeed are put into a line of Reading which may serve 
most effectually to the illustration of the ^Holy Scriptures.* 
Sometimes they are enabled to read the Greek Testament — 
but the general traits of the course of Education for so short 
a period are to fit^" the young Men for a ^useful & respectable 
Christian Ministry.* The friends of the General Baptist 
Interest^^ will not be backward therefore to patronise an In- 
stitution so well calculated to promote the cause of '^God & 
Truth* amongst them.

The case of Mr. Vidler's Church consisting of 250 
Members baptised by immersion & admitting to Communion

" Altered at once from, appoint. 

" Altered at once from, they. 

" The importance of this decision will be apparent in the proceedings of 
next year. It may be viewed from many sides. First, the point was raised, 
apparently for the first time, whether a church could be admitted which com- 
prised any unbaptized members ; and the admission was marked by an express 
reservation on this point. Second, the Assembly recognized the importance of 
the occasion, and appealed to the churches themselves, instead of voting by 
repre.sentatives : of such referendums there are subsequent cases. Third, a 
unanimous vote was not required ; but afterwards we find it mentioned when 
there was no opposition. Fourth, the New Connexion here missed its 
opportunity: it had 41 churches, and had they chosen to vote, their votes 
would apparently have been counted. As it was, only eighteen churches 
replied to the query, though twenty-six were represented at this Assembly by 
letters or representatives. Fifth, this church was avowedly and primarily a 
Universalist church, with no apparent interest in the Six-Principles; its belief 
in universal salvation included of course the leading tenet of the Assembly,

that all men could be saved ; but Vidler was himself a Unitarian, and the 
church was about to be convulsed on the question whether it should follow 
him on this head. Now to many people outside — who proverbially see most 
of the game — this appeared the chief point, and the admission of an avowed 
Unitarian seemed to stamp a new character on the Assembly. Dan Taylor 
had done his best to keep the New Connexion in touch ; he had appealed to 
the Assembly in his early days and his churches had been generously helped ; 
he had responded to their appeals ; he had accepted office at their hands again 
and again ; he was pastor of one of their historic churches. But at this point 
he felt it a sorrowful duty to renounce fellowship, and with the snapping of 
this last tie, the Assembly and the New Connexion went definitely on widely 
diverging paths.

'' The relation of the " Leicestershire Association," as it is steadily styled 
here, namely, the only surviving Association of the New Connexion, had 
always been very peculiar. It met this year in London, not specially as a 
rival to the Assembly, but in due rotation. From this time onward, even the 
shadow of any link disappears. Dan Taylor had appeared as its representa- 
tive, not as representative of the church over which he was pastor — the old 
Goodman's Fields Church — another remarkable instance of holding aloof from 
old friends. Of course, this final rupture was precipitated by the vote of 
Assembly objecting to the title " Free-grace,'' and by its admitting Vidler, who 
had been touring close to the Leicester district with very different doctrines, 
and by its now admitting his church with its Universalist-Unitarian-Paedo- 
baptist membership. The New Connexion had apparently henceforth no 
relation with the Assembly, but often admitted single churches which dropped 
off from it. Vidler, on the other hand, almost at once took the same com- 
manding position in the Assembly that Dan Taylor enjoyed in the New Con- 
nexion, and imparted to it a new tinge. 

'^ The first time the adjective, Old, was used. So much property was 
passing into the hands of the New Connexion, that it was desirable to define 
carefully, but in this case uselessly. 

" The word, thereon, follows and is cancelled. 

™ The building was put up in 1695, behind St. Benedict Square, and was 
vested in trustees during 1726. Forty years later it was lent to the Particular 
Baptists, and in 1781 was let to them on lease for 40 years. John Roberts, of 
Lincoln, was asked, as an afterthought, to collect for the Education Fund this 
year. It was an ingenious idea to get William Richards, the hyper-Calvinist, 
'n touch with the Assembly, to go and preach to the Calvinists in the leased 
General Baptist building ; but next year reveals that he was not welcome. 
The subsequent history is remarkable. In 1819, the New Connexion got hold 
of the property and, on the expiration of the lease in 1821, let it to the Inde- 
pendents ; but forming a new church next year, they soon resumed use of the 
building. A Kingsford built a new edifice in the suburb of Newport, and 
quitted the Connexion with it. The next pastor resigned after long quarrels. 
After a brief pastorate of John Craps, the cause almost collapsed, but was 
reorganised in i860 and joined the G.B. Association. After rebuilding twice, 
the church is represented to-day by the Thomas Cooper Memorial.

" This was an amazing request. Deacon was pastor of the old Leicester 
church, now in the New Connexion, and his sermon was probably a last 
attempt to keep the Assembly on the old lines. But this magazine was 
founded " as an Antidote against the Anti-Christian Doctrine of Endless 
Misery,'' and was edited byVidler, who was, of course, now adding Unitarianism 
to its liberal programme. 

^ This minute was at first omitted, and was supplied on the page opposite, 
which is otherwise unused. 

'^ Robert Aspland was just of age. He had already passed through 
remarkable changes like Vidler. Bred in the Particular Baptist church of 
Soham, where Andrew Fuller had upheld Calvinism, he had been baptised at 
Devonshire Square, the chief London Particular Baptist church. But after 
studying at Bristol and Aberdeen, he had been expelled for his doctrinal views, 
and had come into touch with Evans at Worship Street, who had procured 
him a call to Newport. This year he plainly avowed himself a Unitarian, and 
within a few months was to settle over the ancient Presbyterian church at Old 
■Gravel Pit, Hackney, where for forty years he was to be the champion of 
liberal views in theology and politics. He was speedily associated with Vidler 
in the " Universal Theological Magazine," of which he assumed control by 
1806, changing its name to the " Monthly Repository." 



THE NEW CONNEXION 259 

All relations between the General Assembly and the New Connexion 
were now broken off, although some churches in the one were still linked with 
some churches in the other, to the extent of sharing Pierce Johns' Trust. 
In 1805 the church at Downton, which had just joined the New Connexion, 
asked, What are we to understand by the phrase, " New Connexion of 
General Baptists 1 " To which the official answer was, " Several churches in 
" Lincolnshire, Yorkshire, Kent, &c., in the year 1770, in consequence of a 
'' difference in their religious sentiments, broke off their connexion with the 
" General Baptists, who hold their annual assembly in Worship-street, London. 
" These were joined at the time of their seperation by a body of General 
" Baptists in Leicestershire, Nottinghamshire, Derbyshire, &c., which had 
"recently arisen from small beginnings to a respectable number, and with 
" many churches which afterwards joined them, or were raised by their 
" exertions, have since been distinguished by the appellation of ' The New 
•" Connexion of General Baptists!' " 

On this statement it deserves to be noted (i) That the several churches 
numbered eleven, (2) That in Lincolnshire there were two, one of which 
speedily returned to its old associates, (3) That in Yorkshire there was one, 
Avhich had never as a church been represented at the Assembly, though it had 
been a member of the Lincolnshire Association, and though it had recently 
begged money from the Assembly, (4) That in Kent there were three churches, 
all of which had promptly dropped out of touch, (5) That in Essex there were 
three churches, which also dropped out soon, one returning to the Assembly, 
v(6) That of the two London churches. Church Lane — the old Goodman's Fields 
church — remained semi-isolated, and was not formally received till 1786; 
Gravel Lane — the old Park church — slipped in informally in 1800, whereas the 
church at March was postponed on a point of order that might well have 
delayed its case. It follows then, in the candid words of J. W. Wood, that 
Boston is "the only one of the ancient churches that continued regularly united 
■with the New Connexion from its formation." 

The relations between the New Connexion and the Assembly had been 
.ambiguous. From 1786 till 1802 Dan Taylor had represented the younger 
body at the Assembly, and often signed the minutes where it was recorded as 
the Leicestershire Association. John Deacon also, pastor of Friar Lane, had 
.attended more or less informally. In 1803 Taylor declined to attend, and the 
Association decided not to appoint any one. Feelings were evidently deeply 
moved now, as the case of John Tarratt illustrates. He was one of the 
Leicestershire preachers from 1755, helped form the New Connexion as one of 
the pastors, and laboured at Kegworth till 1799. Then he went to Deal, an 
lOld Assembly church which had joined the New Connexion in 1770 and had 
dropped out within two years, resuming its place at the Assembly. Early this 
year he proposed to come north again, and the Leicestershire Conference 
suggested Ashford or Cauldwell or Epworth or Misterton. But Smedley of 
Retford wrote that he would not be welcome at Misterton or Epworth— all 
feeing originally of the Assembly group, but Retford having passed over while 
Misterton and Epworth still adhered to their old associates. Tarratt therefore 
'went to the Assembly church of Hythe and Folkestone for a few years, then 
went to Sutton Bonington of the New Connexion, as a minister, not pastor. 

It had not been thoroughly settled whether a church could belong to both 
ihe New Connexion and the Assembly. That point was raised in 1806, when 
ithe Association resented the inclusion of Leicester, Bourne, Spalding, &c. in 



26o 



SECOND ASSEMBLY BOOK 



[1804 



the Assembly Letter for 1805, and desired John Deacon to take steps to rectify 
the mistake. 

It may be added that about 1870 deputations of each body were appointed 
frequently to attend the sessions of the other, but not without vehement 
opposition from a minority in the New Connexion. Hopes were entertained 
of a closer union, but the spirit of union produced effects in another direction. 
The teachings of Andrew Fuller had leavened many of the Old Particular 
Baptist churches, which came to see that the gospel was worthy of acceptation 
by all men ; they were reinforced by a multitude of new churches, due 
especially to the influence of Spurgeon. As these were all actively evangelistic, 
at home and abroad, the New Connexion decided in 1891 to practically (though 
not technically) dissolve its organization, and join with these other churches, 
which in many cases were ready to discontinue the epithet "Particular." Since 
then the New Connexion maintains only a legal and formal existence, and 
there is no inducement for it to merge again with the Assembly.

First that none ought to vote but Messengers Elders &

more than one voice & 3ly that Ministers & others not being 
Elders or Representatives of any Church in connection with 
the Assembly tho' we should be glad of their counsel have 
no vote in the business of the Assembly.

^ A Methodist preacher, Henry Poole, had become Baptist, and was 
■chosen Elder of Fleet church, which he took out of the Old Connexion into 
the New in 1770. Moving in 1773 to Lutton, he founded a new church, 
"which joined the New Connexion. But in 1788 he adopted the Universalist 
views preached by Winchester, and to be further popularized by Vidler. So 
in 1793 he and his church dropped out of the New Connexion. As Vidler had 
lately directed attention in the district to the Assembly, this application was 
natural. It is nearly the only case of a church quitting the New Connexion 
for the Old. The New Connexion records at the time called this church 
" Long Sutton," but the building and the manse are just over the boundary, 
being really in the parish of Sutton St. Nicholas or Lutton, a name contracted 
from Little Sutton. The building was erected, according to Adam Taylor, 
in 1776, by subscriptions raised locally and in the New Connexion. To-day it 
bears the date 1700, a probable reference to the fact that even in 1688 members 
resident here were on the church roll of Spalding. In 1723 the members at 
Fleet and Lutton acted together, rather independently of the Spalding 
members ; in 1762 the technical severance from the main stock took place. 
Though in 1770 the Fleet and Lutton members were represented at the 
formation of the New Connexion, they dropped out next year. The Lutton 
members separated from the Fleet church in 1773 under the guidance of 
Henry Poole, the ex-Methodist who had taken the Fleet and Lutton members 
into the New Connexion. The Fleet church appealed to the Assembly in 
1788, but rejoined the New Connexion in 1812. Some members in 1840 
seceded from it and founded the present Long Sutton church, to be dis- 
tinguished from the Lutton Assembly church.

^ He was at first classified as Elder. 

* There is something peculiar about Deacon's attitude. He was son of a 
prominent New Connexion leader, had been trained under Dan Taylor, and 
invited to this old Assembly church. Lately he had preached for the 
Assembly, and now he appears as representative, while the church is in the 
New Connexion, and after the " Leicestershire Association " has formally 
withdrawn. Moreover, he had been Elder, but appears here as Representa- 
tive. This, however, coincides with the church record, that about 1806 he 
ceased to be pastor for five years.

The Treasurer of the General Baptist Education Society 
established by this Assembly 1794 reports that Mr. George 
Smallfield from the Church at Cranbrook still continues on 
the Society & that we expect a young man of promising 
abilities from Wales after the Midsummer Vacation. It is 
a circumstance gratifying to the supporters and well wishers 
of our Society that its finances *are of late more flourishing 
& that thus we are able in a degree to answer the demands 
made upon us by the different Churches in our connection. 
We trust that the same active liberality in support of the 
Institution will continue & even increase — persuaded that its 
advantages will in a few years be sensibly felt amongst us. 
Other denominations amongst the Protestant Dissenters are 
more zealous than ever in extending the aids of education 
to those young Men who are trained up for the Christian 
Ministry. Let us not be behind them in such laudable 
exertions. At least our efforts should be. commensurate with 
our ability & through* the use of such rational means 
sanctioned by the blessing of Heaven our Cause (which we 
believe to be the Cause of God' & of Truth') will diffuse itself 
throughout the World. —

*A Letter being read relative to the state of our General 
Baptist Brethren in Wales^* & Mr. William Morris one of

having communicated some information concerning their late 



^ The proceedings at Wisbech are rather hard to understand. In 1785 
the old Assembly church, dating- from 1655, joined the New Connexion, and its 
history from that time is traced by the historians, Adam Taylor and Wood, 
without the faintest indication of that decision being reversed. In 1905, at the 
2Soth anniversary, there is no hint in the historical statement read of any 
division. The old premises in Place's Yard were kept by the church, but in 
1803 a new chapel in Ely Place was built for the church, and opened by Dan 
Taylor; while in 1804 Joseph Jarrom was ordained and remained pastor till 
1838. As this church in 1871 sold the old premises in Place's Yard, it would 
appear that these continued in its possession all the while. But these minutes 
here make it plain that there was a second General Baptist Church, now 
under the care of Richard Wright (and in 1831 under Neil Walker), which 
was now received into the Assembly. The open point is whether this claimed 
any kind of continuity with the earlier, or was an evolution from Fisher's 
Particular Baptist church, or was newly gathered. Richard Wright was one 
of the great Unitarian champions, who already had won Vidler, and through 
him had brought about the recent obvious change in the Assembly. His 
neighbour, John Gisburne, at Soham, was working in the same direction, and 
both were destined to be pastors of the ancient Conigre Church at Trowbridge. 
It seems probable that this church of Wright's was entirely new. It is certain 
that after a few years it formally disclaimed Baptist principles, having 
previously dropped its connection with the Assembly. 

^* This originally continued, and one of the Ministers (Mr. Morris, of 
), from the Principality, &c.

16. We have to regret that several of our Churches are in

confirmed. It was agreed to continue the same resolutions 
for the regulation of the Assembly which have appeared 
in the former Proceedings. — 

We have received no intelligence from America — but 
request the continuance of our correspondence with the 
General Baptists in that part of the World. — 

Every ^Minister is requested to send the Assembly the 
state of the Church as to the Increase* and Decrease* during 
the last year instead of the number of members. — This is 
a request to which we hope our Churches will pay particular 
attention. — 

*The Treasurer of the ^General Baptist Education 
Society* established by this Assembly in 1794 reports that 
*Mr. Bedlington Cook* from Swansea in South Wales & that 
*Mr. William Bricher* from Rolvenden near Tenterden in 
Kent are now on the Society and are making considerable 
improvement in those branches of Education suited to the 
Christian Ministry. It may also be mentioned that *Mr. 
[Long Sutton.] George Smallfield* finished his education at Christmas last 
& is now settled at Sutton in Lincolnshire where his services 
promise to be of considerable use to the General Baptist 
Cause in that part of the Country. It cannot fail of yielding 
gratification to the supporters & well wishers to our Society

"Worship Street] ASSEMBLY 281 

to hear that young Men are thus training up one after 
another for the promoting with greater ability & success 
the cause of Truth & righteousness. Our Churches are 
thus materially benefitted & we are furthering the glorious 
interests of pure & scriptural Christianity. So fully apprised 
are our Calvinistic Brethren of the utility of learning to [CaivinisHc 
Christian Ministers that not content with flourishing 
Academies they are now establishing a Grammar School by 
"way of preparation for the Academy. ^ This should incite us 
to greater zeal & activity in the important business of Edu- 
cation* for the Christian Ministry persuaded that our cause 
is the best of Causes and it ought not to want those efforts 
on our part which when properly directed will ensure its 
wide & lasting diffusion throughout the World I We have 
in times past been too remiss in this particular but already 
feeling the good effects of such an Institution we shall be 

^ Baptists were the pioneers in organised effort to train for the ministry. 
The Presbyterians long flattered themselves that their exclusion from the 
universities was only temporary. Though many ejected ministers eked out 
a living by taking pupils, and some established Academies to prepare them 
for ordination, yet very few of these private ventures obtained any endowment 
or any assurance of continuity. Baptists never had any illusions about being 
comprehended in an Established Church, and though Samuel How had 
emphasized that humane learning was subordinate to the call of the Holy 
Spirit, they early showed that they were not hostile to it in itself. As early as 
l6;j5, several London ministers convened a meeting "to form a plan for the 
providing an orderly standing ministry in the church, who might give them- 
selves to reading and study." Four years later, Edward Terrill, of Bristol, 
devoted large funds to maintain a pastor or teacher at Broadmead, well skilled 
in Greek and Hebrew, who should teach twelve young Baptists for three half 
days each week during two years each. By 1720, at latest, effective work 
began under this bequest, and is still continued at Bristol College. The Con- 
gregationalists followed with a Fund Academy in 1695, which, after amalgama- 
tion with other institutions, is now represented by New College at Hampstead. 
This apparently provoked White's Alley to urge on the General Baptists a 
similar scheme, which seemed to have dropped. In 1751 began a Western 
Congregational Academy. Professor Ward founded his trust three years 
later, with preference to Baptists; the present Airedale College originated 
1756, Brecon in 1757, and the Countess of Huntingdon's (Cambridge) College 
in 1768. In the new century, the present Hackney was founded by Cong^e- 
gationalists, and Rawdon by Baptists, while Cardiff was just beginning this 
year. All these institutions belonged to "our Calvinistic brethren," but there 
were by this time a few Presbyterian-Unitarian colleges, such as Carmarthen, 
Hackney, and Manchester; while Dan Taylor had trained already 14 students 
at Mile End, under the auspices of the New Connexion. The particular 
combined Grammar School and Academy here spoken of was placed at 
Manchester by the Congregationalists ; but the experiment was abandoned in 
four years.

' Above this once appeared, Lincolnshire Association. The deletion of 
this, while other churches and even the " Leicestershire Association " are still 
entered though not represented, suggests that news had arrived that the 
Association was defunct, as the Assembly acknowledged two years later. Of 
the separate churches, Bourn and Spalding had joined the New Connexion in 
1799, with Gamston j Gosberton, Killingholm, Kirton, Maltby, and Wisbech 
had followed Boston even earlier ; Knipton and Peterborough had followed in 
i8oi. Lincoln, we know, was in Calvinistic hands. There remained Burgh 
and Monksthorp, Coningsby and Tattershall, Crowle, Epworth and Butter- 
wick, Fleet, Louth (almost extinct), March, Misterton, Tetney. Deprived of 
their natural centres at Boston, Lincoln, Peterborough, and Spalding, they 
seem not to have kept up their meetings. Ten years later, Adam Taylor had 
obtained possession of their early records.

The Minutes of the last Year's Proceedings were read 
& confirmed. It was agreed to continue the same Resolutions 
for the regulation of the Assembly which have appeared in 
the former Proceedings. 

We have received no intelligence from America at least 
none of an official^ *nature — but request the continuance of 
our correspondence with the General Baptists in that part 
of the World. 

With respect to Waldron Church — Mr. Bennett reports 
that agreeably to the desire of the last Assembly he has 
visited them found them in a very low state nor does there 
seem at present anything to be done but we recommend that 
some neighboring Minister continue to visit them. In the 
mean time we have requested Brethren Pyall & Buss to write 
to Mr. Saunders on the subject. 

As to the Welsh Churches we must again express our 
regret that we have received no communications from them 
but we recommend our Brother Evans to write to Mr. 
^Benjamin Phillips begging him to send us some account 
of the number & state of the Welsh Generl. Baptist Churches 
against our next Assembly. 

Recommended that a Letter be drawn up by Brethren 
J. Evans & J. Marsom — & that this Letter be printed & sent 
to the several Churches who neglect to send either Represen- 
tatives or Letter to the General Assembly wishing them to 
keep up their connection with the Assembly by Letter if 
they are unable to send Representatives. Postage to be 
paid out of the Messenger's Fund. 

Every Minister is requested to send the Assembly the 
state of the Church as to the *increase & decrease during 
the last year instead of the number of Members. This is 
a request to which we hope our Churches (especially after 
they have received the above Letter) will pay particular
