* In 1755 the old Western Association was marked as dissolved. This may 
"be a new one, formed out of Unitarian Baptist congregations, derived 
either from Particular or General Baptist sources. Daniel Jones has a typical 
liistory, which may illustrate the evolution. Hailing from Carmarthenshire, 
member of a Calvinistic Baptist church at Panteg, educated under Caleb 
Evans at Bristol, he settled in 1792 at Back Lane, Swansea. As has been 
already noted, many Calvinistic Baptist churches in the district were inclining 
to Unitarianism, and Jones leaned in this direction. He went in 1800 to the 
Baptist church at Trowbridge, which had originally been in fellowship with 
the Calvinistic Assembly and Association, but had been affected by the Exeter 
movement, had lost a few Trinitarian members in 1736, and had joined the 
General Baptist Western Association, and then the Unitarian Association in 
1794. In this district he was now busy propagating Unitarian views and 
organising. 

^ The Particular Baptist church at George Street, in Hull, had, in 1807, 
lost its minister, James Lyons, because of his Socinian views. He had come 
from Plymouth, where they were rife.

Joseph Hobbs, who had come from Chatham in 1802, after a disruption of the 
old church. He and Edward Sexton, of Chesham, were now busy in the 
district, so that the Ford and Aylesbury causes mentioned below were revived, 
but were taken into the New Connexion.

8- The Treasurer of the General Baptist Education Society 

reports that ^Mr. George Culmer^ Pound' of Dover 'Mr. John 
Williams' Morris^ & 'Mr. William Jones' are now upon the 
Society & will soon be at liberty to settle with those of our 
Churches who are in want of Ministers. We have never 
before had more than two' young Men — this being commen- 
surate with the finances. We trust therefore that having thus 
extended the number of Pupils — the generosity will be like- 
wise extended. Indeed we are every year the more confirmed 
in our opinion that the Education of Young Men for the 
Ministry is a most essential measure in the supporting & 
promoting the General Baptist Connection. It is a certain 
fact that the 'Particular Baptists' & Independents' have near 
a hundred' young Men educating for the Ministry! Hence 
one principal reason of their prosperity in different parts of 
the Kingdom. In this respect therefore let us imitate them. 
Young Men of talents learning & piety are certain of being 
useful in diffusing abroad the Religion of the New Testament 
— Reason & Revelation are equally the gifts of Heaven & 

[Page 328] will finally enlighten & bless the World. *For a list of 
'Annual Subscriptions' to this Institution & of the Collections'

Education Society attached to these Proceedings — & we trust 
that the list will be annually augmenting as its object is

[Page 332.] 



" Ramsgate this year executed extensive repairs, costing ;^I34 los. Sid. 
It was the last sign of energy for some time. The subsequent history is in 
brief ; — On the death of Thomas Christophers, in 1842, it was found needful 
to examine into the state of the church and its funds. Eleven members were 
resident, one at Canterbury and one at Sandwich. A new service was 
instituted in the morning, so that by 1846 an average attendance of three or 
four could be recorded ; and two were baptised in 1844, after the baptistery 
had been repaired. A school was opened in 1848, and by 1855 the total 
membership rose to thirteen, of whom seven were non-resident. The 
Birchington chapel was leased to the Primitive Methodists, and ultimately sold 
as a new Baptist chapel had been built there (by the hyper-Calvinists ?) ; the 
proceeds put the Ramsgate premises again in repair. The endowments were 
invested in a mortgage on Worship Street, and a loan to Sevenoaks, which 
apparently were put into Government stock by J. C. Means. In 1884 the 
eight members formally disbanded, and the ancient cause ceased to exist. All 
the time it had been crippled by the premises being microscopic, and hidden 
away in an obscure court. 

' Originally, Horley or Nutfield. Mr. John Hale ordered six copies of the 
proceedings to be sent to him at Doghurst Farm, near Bletchingly, by Taylor's 
waggon to Knutfield in Surrey. A note to this effect is on the inside of the 
cover of the book.

& confirmed. It was agreed to continue the same regulations 
of the Assembly which have appeared in the^ Proceedings — 
of 1806 and we think it necessary *to repeat our recommen- 
dation respectg. strangers^ from the Country that they be 
introduced to the Chairman of the Assembly — so that every 
propel attention be paid them. 

We are still without intelligence from America but the 
Assembly earnestly requests its members to procure in- 
formation from that quarter. We are happy to find that 
our friend Revd. Mr. Fenn^ still living & hope to hear from 
him on some future occasion respecting the number & state 
of the Generaf Baptists in that remote quarter of the World. 

Brethren Pyall & Buss have visited Waldron Church — 
& we have received a Letter from them from which it appears 
that they have been long declining — about 20 members —

' The name was caught wrongly at first. James Fenn, of Deal, in 1770 
had left the Assembly with his church of 21 members, but had rejoined in 
1783. Eleven years later he went to America with official credentials, and in 
1797 returned, reporting many General Baptists not organised.

" Since 1780 a Particular Baptist church had divided the forces in Cran- 
brook. The new home of the ancient church was on High Street, and when 
the burial-ground at Speldhurst was sold for a garden, some old tombstones; 
were brought here. The church at last dissolved owing to doctrinal troubles,, 
and the proceeds of the chapel — ^^700 — went to help the five nearest General! 
Baptist churches, and the Education Fund.

Society^ reports that *Mr. William Rees Jones* finished 
his studies at Midsummer last' — and ^Mr. John Williams 
Morris^ at Christmas last — and they feel grateful for the 
advantages which they have enjoyed. Mr. Jones is chosen 
Pastor of the Church of Trowbridge lately under the care of 
our worthy Brother Mr. Daniel Jones.* And Mr. Morris is 
settled at Lewes where his labours are acceptable. i" In their 
room we have upon the Society *Mr. John Philpot* of Canter- 
bury & ^Mr. William DobelP of Cranbrook. — We have to 
renew oiir thanks for the support which the ^Education 
Society^ has already received & to state our hope that greater 
efforts than ever will be made for its promotion in the General 
Baptist Connection" Ministers are much wanted for several 
of our Churches are vacant — but want much to be supplied. 
Every other Denomination of Protestant Djssenters is^^ 
sensible of the utility of learning to the proper discharge of 
the duties of the Christian Ministry & their attention is of 
course directed to this matter with an increasing degree of 
zeal & activity. Let us take a lesson from our Brethren on 
this important subject. Maintaining the great doctrine of 
^General Redemption* it is but justice that the Preachers of 
it should have every advantage which may enable them to 
propagate it successfully throughout the World.^ — A List of 
^Annual Subscriptions* to the Institution & of Collections by 
our Churches will be found at the close of these Proceedings 
— & *the increase* of the List* must gratify every well wisher 
to the Gene — *ral Baptist Interest. The Assembly appoints

A Sermon was preached against ^Intolerance & Per- s. 

secution^ by Brother John Evans from the Parable of the defeated.] 
Tares & Wheat^ — Matt 13. from 24. to 30. — 'which will be 
immediately printed — ^having! a reference to the late rejection 
of Lord Sidmouth's Bill^* in the House of Lords.' 

" Lord Sidmouth had obtained a return, showing that, whereas from 1760 
to 1802 there had been 2604 dissenting- ministers licensed, yet in the last seven 
years 1068 more had taken out licenses. He claimed that this was an abuse 
of the law, so that " cobblers, tailors, pig-drivers, and chimney-sweepers " 
could avoid election to parish office or service in the militia. He, therefore, 
proposed to require a certificate from six substantial and reputable house- 
holders as to the qualifications. This is practically the recommendation 
required in Victoria to-day for ministers of any body, who wish to be entitled 
to perform marriages, and many nonconformists supported the bill then. But 
all who were interested in progress, rather than respectability, opposed it 
vehemently, and, of course, General Baptists, with their constant reliance on 
unpaid, untrained ministers, were among the opponents. Robert Aspland 
was a prominent member of the committee which secured the abandonment 
of the proposal. The reaction was thorough, and next year the Five-mile and 
Conventicle Acts were repealed, while the Toleration Act was extended. In 
1813 Unitarians were formally admitted to equal privileges with other dis-

THE GENERAL BAPTIST FUND. 

Occasional allusions have been made to this, but it may be well to 
summarise its story. In 1717 a public fund was raised to promote the 
interest of the baptized churches, especially by maintaining honourable 
ministers and providing for a supply of such. It was proposed to limit 
its advantages to the Particular Baptists only, and though Benjamin 
Stinton forcibly opposed such limitation, it was decided upon. Two 
years later, the church at Paul's Alley was refused as a contributor, 
despite the advocacy of the HoUis family, while John Hollis was the 
treasurer. It was in this year that the Salters' Hall controversy provoked 
such violent feelings among the Baptists as amongst all other dissenters. 

In 1726 therefore, the Paul's Alley ministers with many others, drew 
up a scheme for a Fund not so limited, and the plan is recorded by 
Crosby at IV. 205. It provided for a three year course of study, which 
might be extended to four or five, and for bursaries not exceeding £20 
a year for not more than two years after finishing the prescribed course 
of study. Within the next three years a joint fraternal, which had met 
at the Hannover Coffee House, and then had transferred to the British 
Coffee House, lost practically all its Particular Baptist members, who drew 
off to a younger fraternal meeting first at ihe Gloucester, then at 
Blackwell's. In like manner the Fund of 1726 soon became practically 
a General Baptist Fund, and became so known. Its declared object was 
" for assisting ministers in the country by grants in aid of stipend and 
grants for books." The Fund was administered with care by Managers 
chosen by the contributing churches, nearly all of which belonged to 
the Assembly, as is shown by a letter printed at the close of the 1737 
minutes. The Assembly took formal notice of it in 1738 and 1747, while 
from 1788 its accounts were printed and circulated with the Assembly 
proceedings. 

But while the Calvinists were content with the Particular Baptist 
Fund, the Managers of this General Baptist Fund never drew any line 
between applicants from Assembly churches and from the New Connexion r 
the chief qualification was their need. By 1825 the invested funds had 
grown to £4,000, while £45 was received in subscriptions; 37 ministers 
received money grants, of whom nearly half belonged to Assembly 
churches, while 18 received books, the New Connexion names of Goadby 
and Winks being in the list with typical Assembly names such as Marten, 
and Green. To-day the Midland Baptist College and the Assembly share 
equally with poor ministers and churches of the New Connexion. 

The General Baptist Education Society which was so warmly 
commended in the last few years here recorded, gradually accumulated 
about £500 capital, and about 1825 was receiving from £80 to £100 
annual subscriptions. 

These two instances may show that the old ideals of corporate effort 
were still before the minds of the Assembly churches, and even if their 
resources were diminished or stationary, they were still earnest to 
maintain their ministry and extend their influence. 

30S 



GENERAL BAPTISTS IN AMERICA. 

The occasional allusions to the fortunes of the General Baptists in 
the English colonies till 1776 may be usefully gathered together, and 
interwoven with notices from Professor Vedder's Short History of the 
Baptists, the new and enlarged edition of 1907. From his pages 287 
to 318 the main thread of the following account is taken. 

Except in Pennsylvania, most of the early Baptist colonists were 
General, and it rwas not till about 1742 that the Philadelphia Association, 
adopting the 1677 Baptist revision of the Westminster Confession, ensured 
the prevalence of a Calvinistic type of theology. Since all but one of 
the early churches whose history is known had their nucleus in a few 
men or women who had been Baptists in England, it is somewhat remark- 
able that the links with the Assembly are not more numerous. 

RHODE ISLAND AND VICINITY. 

The church at Providence was founded in 1639 by Roger Williams, 
HoUiman baptizing Roger Williams, who then baptized ten others. 
Williams soon left the church and others joined. In 1652 the church 
divided, the majority constituting a Six-principle church. In 1771 most 
of this church adopted Calvinism, whereupon the General members 
seceded and established a new church, which survives. 

The church at Newport was traditionally formed in 1644, and included 
Mark Lukar, a Calvinist, who had been immersed in January, 1642, at 
London. By 1648 it was a Baptist church and soon included Obadiah 
Holmes, a Baptist from Preston, possibly connected with the Londoner 
who signed the Calvinist Confession of 1646 and the officer at Leith who 
signed in 1651. But by 1656 the Newport church divided, and a Six- 
principle church was constituted under the leadership of William Vaughn, 
on whom hands had been laid, according to the Fourth principle, at 
Providence. This church protested against psalmody, restraints on 
laymen preaching, particular redemption, as well as the neglect of the 
Fourth principle. 

The first pastor of the Newport church, John Clarke, M.D., succeeded 
in obtaining a charter from Charles II. in 1663 which united Rhode 
Island and Providence Plantations into a colony where civil and religious 
liberty were assured. A speedy result was the foundation of two more 
General Baptist churches on the mainland, at North Kingston in 1665, 
Tiverton in 1685. Just across the border of Plymouth Colony was the 
town of Swansea, to whi(;h Miles of Swansea in Wales had taken his 
Baptist church on the accession of Charles; and here in 1685 a fifth 
General Baptist church was formed by secession from it. Over the 
western border, in the colony of Connecticut, at the mouth of the Thames, 
a sixth was founded at Groton in 1705. Off the coast lay Long Island, 
and when this was captured from the Dutch who had repressed all dissent, 
a Baptist began preaching by 1700 so that a church was organized at 

307 



3o8 GENERAL BAPTISTS IN AMERICA 

Oyster Bay in 1724. Two attempts were made in New York, but the 
second expired soon after 1730 by internal dissension — possibly con- 
nected with Calvinist doctrine? Most of the churches in New Jersey 
were originally General Baptist, but they were soon drawn into fellowship 
with the Calvinists of Pennsylvania. 

By 1670 the handful of General Baptist churches had attained a 
definite standing, and before 1729 they had organized a Yearly Meeting. 
It is remarkable that this group of Northern churches never entered into 
relations with the English Assembly. In 1768 the Minutes record that 
Morgan Edwards from Philadelphia presented his credentials, and his 
plan for a college in Rhode Island was formally approved. Now 
Edwards was educated at the Bristol Calvinistic Baptist College, but 
had been to Boston, in Lincolnshire, ministering to a little flock. No 
other cause apparently existed in Boston except the old High Street 
General Baptist church, whose officers signed the Confession of 1651. 
In 1751 died the Elder, John Goode, who was also Messenger for the 
district, and for twelve years there was no Elder, only a succession of 
ministers. This fits exactly with Professor Vedder's statement that 
Edwards was ordained only in 1757 at Cork. He returned to England 
and preached at Rye, oije of the few Kentish towns where there was a 
Calvinistic Baptist church. On the recommendation of Dr. Gill, the 
Calvinist of Southwark, he went to the Philadelphia church. There he at 
once proposed to found a college, and the plan was adopted; a charter 
was obtained in Rhode Island during 1764, and Edwards was sent back to 
England to canvass for support. In this he succeeded so well that he 
took over a large sum of money. In 1770 the premises were erected at 
Providence, and next year the old General Baptist church was won over 
by the president to abandon its historic position. Edwards continued his 
efforts, however, in England, and persuaded Dr. Richards (of Lynn?) to 
bequeath his library of 1300 volumes, which became the nucleus of the 
finest collection of Welsh books in America. 



YIB&INIA ASH NOBTH CABOLIKA 

The Minutes show that in 1702 there were brethren of the Baptist 
persuasion and of the General faith, residing in Carolina; a fact 
apparently not widely known now in America. They sent to the General 
Association- — not the Assembly, but those who protested against the 
opinions of Caifin — desiring to be supplied with a ministry or with books. 
Samuel Keeling of White's Alley obtained £7. 12s. for books and was 
asked to write to them. Unfortunately the records of the Association 
have been destroyed after 1709, and the records of the Assembly at 
Stony Stratford, which is its natural heir, are lost. Only in 1714 do we 
find the London Assembly sending Robert Norden of Warbleton and 
Thomas White as Messengers to Virginia; while subscriptions were made 
throughout the Kentish Association for several years. White died on 
the voyage, as Benedict tells, but Norden gathered a church at Burley, 
in Isle-of-Wight county, a district where Puritans had been strongly 
settled from 1619. In 1724 Norden wrote to the Assembly, and next 
year a committee was appointed to settle whether he should be invited 
to return, and to raise necessary funds. However he died at his post 



GENERAL BAPTISTS IN AMERICA 309 

in 1725. The next pastor was another colonist from England, not sent 
expressly from the Assembly, and he founded a second General Baptist 
church in the adjoining county of Suity. Aibout 1 756, they appealed 
to the Philadelphia Association and probably became Calvinist. 

From these churches, Benedict tells us, colonists went down to North 
Carolina, where by 1727 a church was organized in Perquimans County 
on Albemarle Sound, and in 1742 another in Halifax County, up the 
Roanoke. In 1729 there was so much promise here that a North Carolina 
Association was formed, which by 1752 had sixteen churches. Soon 
afterwards the Philadelphia Association came into touch with it and 
altered its character, so that by 1765 it was reorganized as the Kehukee 
Association. In 1772 the churches resulting from the labours of Whitfield 
were so numerous that the Kehukee Association proposed a conference, 
and the result was the union on a Calvinistic basis. 



SOUTH CAROLINA 

The third centre of the General Baptists was in South Carolina. A 
Calvinistic church had come down from Massachusetts, driven from 
Kittery by the intolerance of the Congregationalists, and had been re- 
inforced from the West of England. In 1699 they erected a meeting 
house in Church Street in Charlestown, the site being given by William 
Elliott. His son became a General Baptist leader, according to the 
tradition given to our friend Morgan Edwards, and quoted from him by 
Benedict; by 1733 a General Baptist church drew off from the original, 
and was strong enough to found a second church at " Stone," which 
seems an error of reading for Stono, ten miles west of Charleston, on the 
Stono River. In 1737 they made an appeal to the Assembly, with the 
result that in 1739 Haywood was sent out as Messenger. There were 
property troubles, resulting in the Charlestown meeting house being 
awarded to the General Baptists. In 1756 this church applied again to 
the Assembly, guaranteeing £70 yearly; next year they raised the 
guarantee to £100, sending £50 as an earnest of good faith. Grantham 
Killingworth of Norwich interested himself, and by 1758 Daniel Wheeler 
was sent out. Letters continued for five years, the later ones being from 
Stono. In 1772 a further application was made, and next year Philip 
Dobell was sent out to Charlestown. Here the veil drops, and when the 
colonists had vindicated their independence, we find that the Calvinists 
had come into peaceable possession of the whole property, so that 
presumably the General Baptists were negligible here by 1787. 

MARYLAND. 

A fourth centre afterwards came into touch with the Assembly by 
the despatch of James Fenn in 1794. By 1798 letters came as to two 
buildings, at Fell's Point in Baltimore, and at Deer Creek. But these 
were really daughters of the New Connexion, being composed of emig- 
rants from the Friar Lane church at Leicester, which had ordained John 
Healey as minister before his departure in 1794. Their letters were sent 
to the New Connexion, and after 1802 the Assembly could get no replies 
from them. 



3IO GENERAL BAPTISTS IN AMERICA 

It will be observed that the Caffinite controversy did not spread to 
the colonies. And while the decline of fervour during the eighteenth 
century affected the English General Baptists, leading most of them to 
the Unitarian position, that was not the case in America. The great 
revival there due to Jonathan Edwards and Whitfield created hundreds 
of new Calvinistic Baptist churches, but on the Arminian side there arose 
only Methodist churches from which no Baptists seem to have arisen 
as in England, unless the Free-Will Baptists are to be accounted thus. 
Therefore the old General Baptists were left in backwaters, and to-day 
are not serious factors in the religious life of the country. The old 
original group, dating in Rhode Island from 1639, has only 18 churches 
and not 1,000 members in New England and Pennsylvania. Its off- 
shoots in England are about of the same importance; and have been 
alluded to at I. Ivi. The North Carolina group was not all won over to 
Calvinism, and may still be found under the title. Original Free-Will 
Baptists. Professor Vedder quotes them as having in 1890, in both 
Carolinas, 167 churches, with 11,864 members. They appear to uphold 
largely the doctrines and practices of 1660.

INDEX TO PLACES. 



All the names are given in alphabetical order, spelt as in Bar- 
tholomew's Gazetteer of 1904. Cross references are given from ex- 
ceptional forms habitual in the Minutes. A classified county list will 
be found at I. Ivi., and a shorter one at II. x: the former presents a 
condensed view of the church history to the present century, including 
other references than those in the Minutes, these are not repeated here. 
As several churches changed their meeting places, the actual home is 
given for each period, with references to the preceding and succeeding 
places : a special note as to this is given at I. 1. and Iv. (A few churches 
which did not belong to the Assembly are also indexed in brackets.) 
Living churches in the Assembly are starred, e.g. *Bessels Green. [The 
present affiliations of living churches not now in the Assembly are 
added in square brackets.] In a few instances, by a species of re- 
action, hyper-Calvinistic Baptist churches have arisen in the same villages, 
as in Cuckfield, Smarden. No notice is taken of these. The century 
number is not repeated; thus 1701, 05-09, 33 means 1701, 1705-1709, 
1733-

BAPTIST HISTORICAL SOCIETY. 

The Society invites the adhesion of all who are interested 
in a history which extends over three centuries. The Annual 
Meeting is held in London at the time of the Baptist Union 
meetings, about the en4 of April. Three copies of the Trans- 
actions are issued yearly, in April,. September, and January; 
these are supplied to all who subscribe five shillings. 

Larger works are published also; the present volumes 
of the Minutes of the General Baptists represent issues for 
1908-9, 1909-10. These are supplied, as well as the Trans- 
actions, to all subscribers of a guinea. A composition of 
ten guineas qualifies for life. Future issues of this description 
will probably include the texts of many early Baptist Con- 
fessions, and a study of several early churches in North- 
amptonshire and the neighbourhood. 

A list of the chief articles in the first four issues of the 
Transactions is presented, to show the scope of the Society's 
work : — 

Leonard Busher, Dutchman; 161 1 — 1642. 
Origin of the Particular Baptists in London; 161 6 — 1644. 
Sutton-in-the-Elms and Arnesby; 1650— 1800. 
Porton in Hampshire; 1655 — 1685. 
May a Minister take State Pay? 1656. 
Baptists and Bartholomew's Day; 1662. 
Bromsgrove; 1666 — 1698. 
Militant Baptists; 1660 — 1672. 
Early Welsh Baptist Doctrines; jaefore 1670. 
The Baptist Licences; 1672 — 1673. 
An Unrecorded First Edition of Bunyan; 1675. 
Baptist Literature till 1688; A— Br. 
Old Wisbech Records; 1700— 1837. 

Original Sin, Feetwashing, and the New Connexion; 177 1. 
William Vidler, Baptist and Universalist ; 1758— 18 16. 
Letters to Dr. Rippon from New York; 1800. 
Letters of Dr. Carey to his sons; 1812, 18 14. 
Dr. Carey and the Baptist Magazine; 18 15. 
Printing the Funeral Sermon of Dr. Ryland; 1825. 
First Impressions of Bristol; 1826. 
The Serampore Mission; 1836.

William Allen as one whom the Lord has in a good 
Measure gifted and qualified for that Work and as 
this is an Office of a general Emploiyment so it is that 
wherein all Churches are concem'd to look out and 
send forth Men of suitable Minds and Abilities that they 
may be indeed ye Messengers of the Churches and 
Glory of Christ and consequently all Churches wherei 
such Men are found must in Compliance to the Gospel 
Rule give up such Members to that Service wn. called 
forth thereto So that we hope the Church will not 
think it a greater Hardship to give up such Member 
to the Lords Work than other Churches who are to do 
the Same & therefore readily comply wth. our Request. 

And whiereas our Bro. Allen questions his own 
Ability for that great Work we like him never the worse 
for his modesty therein yet we think 'tis not himself 
*that is to be the only Judge of Qualification that the 
Churches who are to take Care whom they set apart to 
that Work & Business and if they think him fit we 
hope he will the more readily submit and comply wth. 
their Choice & Purpose. 

And whereas our Bro. Allen thinks he ought to 
be first a Presbyter ye better to prepare him foT a 
Messenger as we find no such Rule in ye Gospel to 
proceed so on the contrary ^as we find'' our Lord gives 
the different Gifts to the Members of his Church to fit 
them for the different Employments they are to engage 
in as Eph. 4. 11. so where the Churches sees those 
different Gifts appear there they are to place the Office 
as thereby directed by Christ in the Gifts he has given 
them to fit them for the same. 

And lastly as our beloved Bro. Allen desires to be 
satisfied that this Office is of Divine Appointment & 
as such is to be continued in the Churches to Satisfie 
himself herein so we hope he will remember that our 
Lord having set up such an Office in his Church telling 
them that the Gospel of ye Kingdom must be preached 
to all Nations till the End come Mat. 24. 14, And 
accordingly has promised his Presence wth. them to the 
End of the World Mat. 28. ult, And as the first Apostles 
had their Successors so our Lord is said to furnish his 
Church with this as well as other Gifts for the work 
of ye Ministry & perfecting of the Saints till the whole
